# SUBMISSION NOTES (remove before submitting)

This block is for the author only. Delete everything from this heading to the line "END OF SUBMISSION NOTES" before upload. Items marked **UNCONFIRMED** could not be checked.

## A. Target journal

- **International Journal of Astrobiology (IJA), Cambridge University Press.** ISSN 1473-5504 (print), 1475-3006 (online). It is a single-anonymous peer-reviewed, fully Gold Open Access journal.
- **Scope fits.** The Aims and Scope list includes "Cosmic prebiotic chemistry", "Origins, evolution and distribution of life" and "Life Detection in our solar system and beyond".
- **Sources.** I fetched every item below from the live Cambridge Core pages on 2026-10-11:
  - https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/international-journal-of-astrobiology/information/author-instructions/preparing-your-materials
  - .../information/author-instructions/submitting-your-materials
  - .../information/author-instructions/fees-and-pricing
  - .../information/journal-policies/open-access-options
  - .../information/journal-policies/publishing-ethics
  - .../information/journal-policies/research-transparency
  - .../information/about-this-journal
- **Why not OLEB.** This draft replaces an earlier one aimed at *Origins of Life and Evolution of Biospheres*. That journal became Springer's *Discover Life* on 1 January 2024. Its scope is now broad life science, so it fits this paper poorly.

## B. Author guidelines and how this draft follows them

| Item | IJA guideline | This draft |
|---|---|---|
| Submission | ScholarOne, http://mc.manuscriptcentral.com/submit_ija , with a covering letter or email. Authors may suggest up to three referees. | Cover letter not written. |
| Article types | Research Articles and Review Articles. "There are no page charges or length restrictions." | **Research Article.** |
| Length | No limit. | The main text (Introduction to Conclusions, including the six figure captions, the tables and the display equations) is about 21,600 words (whitespace count, 2026-10-16; about 15,000 at the 2026-10-12 sweep pass, 12,067 before it). With back matter and references (this block excluded) it is about 26,000 words (about 18,400 at the sweep pass, 15,280 before). |
| Title page | A concise title, author name(s), full institutional addresses, and the corresponding author's name, address, telephone number and e-mail. | Placeholders for address, phone and e-mail. **Fill these in.** |
| Abstract | One paragraph, at most 300 words, giving aims, results and conclusions for a wide audience. | One paragraph of 300 words (whitespace count, 2026-10-25, paragraph trims; 300 at the read-through fixes; 299 on 2026-10-21, E117; 298 at the 2026-10-12 sweep pass, 300 after step 4 in E58, 317 after the bias-range widening of E77–E79 on 2026-10-13, and 319 before E117). Within the limit. |
| Keywords | **Do not put keywords on the manuscript.** They are entered at submission. The two guideline pages disagree: "preparing your materials" says choose two from a list and optionally add a third; "submitting your materials" says a free-text box with at least three. **UNCONFIRMED which applies.** | None in the manuscript. Suggested: homochirality; parity violation; ocean worlds; icy moons; Frank model; prebiotic chemistry. |
| File format | Word (manuscript.doc, or manuscript.rtf on a Mac), or the IJA LaTeX template (manuscript.tex, figures fig1.eps ...). Equations in MathType or Equation Editor, not as images. | This is Markdown with LaTeX equations. **Convert it with the IJA LaTeX template, or to Word with live equations.** |
| Units, abbreviations | Metric and SI units. Define unfamiliar abbreviations at first use. | Done. |
| Equations and tables | Equations numbered (1), (2) ... Tables titled and numbered Table 1 ... | Done. |
| Figures | Embed figures in the manuscript for review. Each has one caption beneath it and is cited in the text in order. After acceptance, supply EPS or TIFF (1200 dpi line art, 300 dpi halftone or colour in CMYK, 600 dpi mixed; lines at least 0.5 pt). Colour is free. Refer to all illustrations as "Figures". | Six figures, each with a caption, numbered in order of first mention: journals/fig_schematic2.png, sketch.png, threshold2.png, coarsening.png, journals/fig_sensitivity3.png and shares3.png (scripts in the code table). Figures 1, 3, 5 and 6 are saved at 300 dpi, which meets the halftone and colour requirement but not the 600 dpi one for mixed art. **At acceptance all six must be re-exported as EPS or TIFF** from the same scripts, at 600 dpi if treated as mixed art. |
| Accessibility | WCAG 2.1 AA. Alt-text for every figure, sent on the Accessibility Descriptions Submission Form. Machine-generated alt-text is used if none is given. | **Write the alt-text at acceptance.** Check the colour contrast of Figure 5. |
| References | Harvard (Author, date) in the text. The list is alphabetical, includes article titles, and uses unabbreviated journal titles. Example format: "Burbidge, E.M., Burbidge, G. R., Fowler, W. A., Hoyle, F. 1957, Synthesis of the Elements in Stars, Rev. Mod. Phys., 29, 547-650". Unpublished work is cited in the text only ("private communication"), unless in press. | Harvard, alphabetical, with full titles and full journal names from Crossref. DOIs are added as https://doi.org/ links (the guideline neither asks for nor forbids them). Lists with more than 10 authors are cut to the first 10 plus "et al." **UNCONFIRMED whether IJA requires every author.** |
| Acknowledgements | At the end, before References. List all funding sources. | Present. States no funding. |
| Competing interests | Required in the main file. With none: "Competing interests: The author(s) declare none." | Present, in that wording. |
| Data | Data Availability Statements are encouraged. Use repositories with permanent identifiers. | Data and code statements present. **Consider archiving a tagged release on Zenodo for a DOI.** |
| Supplementary material | Allowed. At most 10 MB per file. | None. |
| ORCID | Encouraged. | **Add one if you have it.** |

### AI policy (preparing-your-materials page, "Use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) Tools")

- AI tools cannot be authors.
- Declare any use to generate text or to analyse data. Minimal uses, such as spelling checks, need no declaration.
- Where to declare it:
  - text generation goes "in a separate acknowledgments section or in a footnote";
  - use for data collection or analysis goes in Methods;
  - use for images goes in the figure caption.
- What to include:
  - the tool's name and version;
  - the dates of use;
  - how others can access it;
  - a full description of the use;
  - citations for any third-party material in the output.
- The draft declares the code and analysis use in Methods (Section 2.8) and the writing use in Acknowledgements.
- **Filled in from the git history (2026-10-16), to confirm:** the commit trailers name Claude Opus 5.5 (230 commits, 155 of them the 1M-context version), Claude Sonnet 5 (15), Claude Sonnet 5.5 (4) and Claude Fable 5.1 (1), from 2026-09-23 to 2026-10-21 (recounted 2026-10-21, E117). Section 2.8 and the Acknowledgements now give these with a [CONFIRM] marker: add any use outside Claude Code (for example claude.ai chats) and the final end date.
- No figure was generated by AI. The plotting scripts were written with AI help (see Section 2.8).

## C. Fees: no free route unless a waiver is granted (live fees page, 2026-10-11)

- IJA is "a wholly open access journal". Every article is Gold OA under a Creative Commons licence.
- **APC: GBP 2610 / USD 3655** (taxes may apply). There are no page charges and no colour charges.
- Funding routes:
  - institutional OA agreements (check whether Cupertino High School has one; probably not);
  - the Cambridge Open Equity Initiative, for over 100 low- and middle-income countries (the USA is not one);
  - Research4Life;
  - **discretionary waivers.** Authors "must request a waiver at or before submission", using the waiver request form.
- Editors and reviewers play no part in funding decisions.
- **This work has no funding. File the waiver request with the submission.** Without a waiver, there is no free subscription route.

## D. Prior publication

- Per advisor review (2026-10-11): not submitting to RNAAS or any preprint server; full paper to IJA only.

## E. Number conflicts between sources (not resolved silently)

1. **Ozturk et al. 2023: two different papers.** RNAAS_DRAFT.md and limits_lit.md cite *Nat. Commun.* 14:6351 (doi:10.1038/s41467-023-42130-8, avalanche magnetization of magnetite by an RNA precursor). PAPER_DRAFT.md cites *Sci. Adv.* 9:eadg8274 (doi:10.1126/sciadv.adg8274, crystallization of an RNA precursor on a magnetic surface). Both are real, and their Crossref titles match. I cite both:
   - (2023b) *Sci. Adv.*: the enantioselective crystallization on magnetite, used for planned-experiment arm D;
   - (2023a) *Nat. Commun.*: magnetization of magnetite, as in RNAAS.
   - **Check which paper reports the ~60% excess** that PAPER §3.13 quotes. The draft cites both for that sentence.
   - *2026-10-16:* answered in the text: Section 1.1 now cites Ozturk et al. (2023b), the crystallization paper, alone for about 60% ee (100% after recrystallization). The author should confirm the value against that paper.
   - *2026-10-21:* confirmed from the paper's text: about 60% is the best run; repeats give 59, 36 and 18% (down-spin, mean 38%) and −10 to −54% (up-spin, mean −32%), about 35% on average by the authors' count. Section 1.1 now says so (E115).
2. **Magnetite threshold.** RNAAS gives "less than about 2–4 × 10⁻¹⁵" for Europa and Enceladus. PAPER §3.13 gives "below 1.9×10⁻¹⁵" for early Earth. These are different worlds, and each value is used only for its own world.
3. **Allen–Cahn prefactor.** PAPER §3.3 gives A = 5.48 and 5.60 (corrected 2026-09-23 from a rounded "5.5 at both"). The PAPER Fig. 2 caption and the coarsening.png legend still say "l = 5.5·√(D t)", and the sampling code uses the rounded A = 5.5 (`coarsen3d.py`, `uncertainty.py`). The text uses 5.48 and 5.60, states that the sampling uses 5.5 (Section 2.4), and the Figure 4 caption calls the dashed line the rounded fit. **The legend in the image still reads 5.5.**
4. **Coarsening shares (not a conflict).** The 99% in the Figure 4 caption is the 96³ figure run (0.989 at t = 400, coarsen_fig.log). The 93.0% in Section 3.2 (Section 2.4 before the 2026-10-12 pass) is a separate 128³ run (coarsen3d.log). Each is now quoted with its grid size.
5. **Europa without a lid.** RNAAS and PAPER give 29%; ocean_worlds.log gives 28.6%. This is rounding. I use 29%, as RNAAS does.
6. **Bennu table.** PAPER §3.10 gives the step rule (15%/21%/57% and 17%/22%/55%) and the full quench curve (16%/21%/57% and 17%/23%/57%). These are two methods, not a conflict. I give the step rule and say that the quench method agrees within 2 points, as PAPER does.

7. **Vertical healing rule.** The `uncertainty.py` docstring states a vertical healing condition (H/A)²/D_z ≤ τ, but `sweep()` does not apply it. Section 2.6 explains why it is implied in case (i). No numerical effect.
8. **Column onset for Enceladus.** `aniso3d.log` gives D_z < D_h/333; the code asserts 334 within 5%. Section 2.3 uses 333.
9. **Europa S\*.** The draft and `strat_sweep.py` use 3.0 g/kg under 20 km of ice; VERIFICATION.md gives 3.2 g/kg at 24.2 MPa and 2.55 g/kg at about 25 MPa. The `strat_sweep.py` docstring says "~25 MPa, 20 km ice", while `verify_sstar.py` uses 24.2 MPa. The text keeps 3.0 and Table 2 reports the 3.2 recomputation.
10. **Ice density and gravity.** The draft uses ρ_ice = 920 kg/m³; `verify_sstar.py` uses 917 kg/m³ with g_s = 1.315 m/s². The text keeps 920 and does not state Europa's gravity. **State g_s in Section 2.5 or confirm it.** *Resolved by E96:* the sampling code uses 920 kg/m³ × 1.315 m/s² (`rerun_base.py:511`, `robust_ranking.py:42`, `lid_melt.py:104`); Section 2.5 now states g_s = 1.315 m/s² for Europa. `verify_sstar.py` takes the resulting 24.2 MPa directly, so it uses the same pressure; the 917 kg/m³ in its comment is a slip (917 × 1.315 × 20 km = 24.1 MPa).
11. **Lid cutoff.** Section 2.5 gives "about 28 MPa", `verify_sstar.py` finds no S\* above 28–29 MPa, and Section 3.5 gives "about 23 km of ice". These agree if g_s ≈ 1.3 m/s²; the text states both forms.
12. **Normal-form check tolerance.** The draft says the 4,000-run check agrees within ±0.005; `sim.py: main()` asserts agreement within 0.03. Table 2 now gives both.
13. **Bias range.** Table 1 gives g from 4×10⁻¹⁹ to 4×10⁻¹⁶; the `review.py: r1_scale` comment says "~1e-19..1e-16". The text uses Table 1.
14. **Scale-rule range.** "10³³–10³⁴ molecules" is not tied to a stated range of g and kτ. The values 3.6–6.7×10³³ come from T = 0–100 °C at g = 10⁻¹⁷ and kτ = 10 (Section 3.1; Section 2.2.4 before the 2026-10-12 trim).
15. **Equation renumbering (checked; no unmatched references).** The old Eqs. 1–4 were remapped to the new numbering 1–21: old Eq. 1 (normal form) → Eq. 6; old Eq. 2 (P = Φ(Δ)) → Eqs. 10 and 11 (Eq. 11 is the form in N); old Eq. 3 (field equation) → Eq. 14; old Eq. 4 (the display N ≥ … in Section 3.1) duplicated Eq. 13 and was removed, so Section 3.1 now cites Eq. 13. Old reference "Eq. 2" in the code table now reads "Eqs. 1 and 6–13".

46. **Three-way split (new; Sections 3.3, 4, 6).** Chance sets the hand in at most 14.9% of draws in any world (Umbriel, no lid; `multiwall.log:526`); Enceladus 8.8, Europa 4.9, Earth 0.0 (`multiwall.log:539, 535, 525`). The PVED's share of single-hand outcomes, P_pved/(P_pved + P_chance), is 100% for Earth, 89% Europa, 76% Enceladus and 74% at the minimum (Mimas), computed from `multiwall.log:525–539`.

Munk and Wunsch (1998) was read in full on 2026-10-13: their Eq. 3.15, D = gγ⁻¹κAΔρ, is the K_z formula above solved for the dissipation; they adopt γ = 0.2 as Osborn's (1980) upper bound (p. 1986), so the Γ = 0.3 end of the range lies above that bound; their Question 8 (p. 2001) cites γ = 0.05 in a salt-stabilized fjord (Stigebrandt and Aure, 1989), four times below the mid value and not covered by the 0.1–0.3 range. Section 2.6 now says Γ = 0.2 is Osborn's bound as adopted by Munk and Wunsch and that lower values would lower K_z in proportion. Osborn (1980) itself was not read (closed access; the publisher site refused the in-app browser): it is cited at second hand through Munk and Wunsch. See H31.

65. **Full-text checks, H7 (2026-10-13; `journals/fulltext_check_a.md`, `journals/fulltext_check_b.md`).** No model number changed. Section 1.1: the [CHECK FULL TEXT] intro sentence removed. Vester et al. (1959) and Ulbricht and Vester (1962): mechanism (polarized beta electrons → circularly polarized bremsstrahlung) and null result (no optical activity above 0.02% in about ten reactions) from Bonner (1974, NASA NTRS preprint read in full), added to the references. Yamagata (1966): the "tiny bias that accumulates through repeated steps or amplification" summary, which Wesendrup et al.'s abstract does not support, replaced by Rein's (1974, p. 16) description as an accumulation mechanism over geological time. Rein (1974): from his own Summary (p. 15, free preview). Letokhov (1975): the infrared-line proposal, as described by Quack et al. (2022, §4) and Darquié et al. (2010). Kondepudi and Nelson: "already estimated the volume and time" (Sections 1.1 and 4) → "already argued that such a bias needs a large system and a long time"; the lake-sized body over 10⁴–10⁵ years is credited to Quack et al. (2022); the 15,000 years is from the 1985 abstract. Bonner (1991): scope only, from its table of contents. Avalos et al. (2000): sentence and reference cut (closed, no abstract, cited nowhere else). Wesendrup et al. (2003): abstract result. Cowan and Furnstahl (2022): reworded from the arXiv full text (formose step, alkaline-earth ions, no fluctuations). Section 4 Viedma bullet: "always set the outcome" → "went to 100% of the same hand within 8 h" (the paper reports averages of more than 20 runs; arXiv cond-mat/0407479v5, Fig. 5). Reference list: +Bonner 1974, −Avalos 2000, net 0. Every DOI checked on Crossref.

70. **Szabó-Nagy and Keszthelyi (1999) (Section 1.1, "Doubts about the size and the sign").** After "The PVED has still not been detected experimentally." added three sentences: the chapter reports crystals from racemic cobalt and iridium complexes slightly richer in one hand, which the authors read as support for the PVED; I treat it as an unconfirmed claim, not a detection (signals near the spectrometer's sensitivity limit, p. 369; crystal-growth exponent x unknown, so the effect size was inferred by assuming a theoretical PVED, p. 374; the introduction says experimental confirmation is still missing, p. 367). → Chapter 28, pp. 367–376, read in full 2026-10-13, as data only. No number changed; the chapter's own ε values for the metal complexes (8.3×10⁻¹⁴ and 4.47×10⁻¹¹, from a Z⁶ scaling) are not used.
71. **Keszthelyi (1999) (Section 1.1, same paragraph, after MacDermott 1995).** Added: a short review of the preceding few years gives the amino-acid PVED as about 10⁻¹⁷ kT with the L form lower, notes that newer calculations raise it by one to two orders of magnitude, calls it questionable that so small a bias could push a developing system, and puts the lack of convincing experiments down to the weak effect in carbon-centred molecules (pp. 101–102). → Chapter 8, pp. 99–103, read in full 2026-10-13. His 10⁻¹⁷ kT lies inside the draft's 4×10⁻¹⁸ to 3×10⁻¹⁶ kT; no number changed.
72. **Zanasi et al. (1999) (Section 1.1, same place).** Added: five L-amino acids, hydrated D-glyceraldehyde and D-camphor, non-relativistic coupled-method approximations; the amino-acid shifts change sign with the dihedral angle and the largest negative value is at the conformation preferred in water; glyceraldehyde is negative at every angle in the best calculation; the authors report values about ten times larger than earlier ones (Tables 3–8, pp. 381–385). → Chapter 29, pp. 377–385, read in full 2026-10-13. No number changed. **Flag for the author [resolved by E77]:** the chapter defines the PVED as twice its tabulated shift. With that, the extended-basis RPA values for the preferred conformations are about 7.2 aeV (alanine), 7.7 (glycine), 7.5 (aspartate), 9.0 (valine), i.e. g = 3.1–3.8×10⁻¹⁶ at 273 K, and the largest tabulated valine value (any angle) is 11.4 aeV (4.8×10⁻¹⁶). The draft's range tops out at 6.2 aeV (2.64×10⁻¹⁶, alanine, Quack et al., 2022), so these are 1.2–1.8 times above it. If the shift is used without the factor 2 (the chapter's own conversion, 10⁻²⁰ hartree ≈ 10⁻¹⁷ kT), they are 1.0–2.4×10⁻¹⁶ and inside the range. The sentence added says nothing about the draft's range, and the range and Table 1 are unchanged; whether to widen the upper end is left open. These are uncorrelated non-relativistic values; Berger and Quack (2000), already cited, judged earlier claims of this kind unjustified.
73. **Zanasi et al. (1999) (Section 2.1, conformer averaging).** "(Tranter et al., 1992)" → "(Tranter et al., 1992; Zanasi et al., 1999)" after "whose individual values can differ in sign". → Tables 3–7 of Chapter 29 (the amino-acid shifts change sign with the dihedral angle). Text only; no number changed.
74. **Avetisov (1999) (Section 1.1, after Bonner 2000).** Added: a kinetic objection. A weak chiral field can purify a monomer pool if its advantage is large compared with racemization (Eq. 1 of the chapter, p. 70), but a medium made pure that way cannot stay pure as enantiospecific functions arise, and purity from spontaneous symmetry breaking among simple molecules needs catalysts about as selective as enzymes; the author concludes that chiral physical factors cannot be of significant consequence, while saying this does not rule out an asymmetric origin of life (pp. 78–82). → Chapter 6, pp. 69–83, read in full 2026-10-13. No number changed. Not added to the limitations list: my model ends when one hand fills the mixed volume, so this step lies outside it; the author may want a sentence there.
75. **Four references (Section F, reference list).** Avetisov 1999, Keszthelyi 1999, Szabó-Nagy and Keszthelyi 1999 and Zanasi et al. 1999 added in alphabetical places, in the format of the Lunine et al. (1999) entry (E24). DOI, title, authors and pages checked on Crossref on 2026-10-13: 10.1016/B978-008043404-9/50005-X (pp. 69–83), /50007-3 (pp. 99–103), /50027-9 (pp. 367–376), /50028-0 (pp. 377–385), all matching the chapter PDFs. List now 123 entries, 122 with DOIs. `check_citations.py` after the edits: 123 entries, every entry cited, every citation listed, apart from the known Rein 1980 and Wesendrup 2000 flags.
76. **Limitations bullet for Avetisov (1999) (Section 4, after "The bias itself is uncertain").** Added at the author's request (2026-10-13), following E74: the model stops once one hand fills the mixed volume; whether that hand lasts is not modelled; Avetisov's objection concerns that later step. New text, no number changed.

77. **Bias range widened to include Zanasi et al. (1999) (2026-10-13; `g_wide.py`, `g_wide.log`; resolves the flag in E72).** Author's decision: read PVED = 2 × the chapter's tabulated shift (p. 377). Stated published range: Section 2.1 "the published amino-acid range of 0.10 to 6.2 aeV" → "0.10 to 6.2 aeV … The published range is therefore 4.4×10⁻¹⁸ to 4.8×10⁻¹⁶" (the sampled range is unchanged); Section 1.2 "about 4×10⁻¹⁸ to 3×10⁻¹⁶ kT" → "about 4×10⁻¹⁸ to 5×10⁻¹⁶ kT"; Table 1 bias row gains "published values reach 4.8×10⁻¹⁶ (11.4 aeV)"; abstract gains "the widest published calculation reaches 11.4 aeV". → conversion `g_wide.log:3–10` (re-derived from the extended-basis RPA columns of Tables 3–7: glycine, alanine, aspartate, valine preferred conformations 7.2–9.0 aeV = 3.1–3.8×10⁻¹⁶; valine at one angle 11.4 aeV = 4.825×10⁻¹⁶; serine 4.96 aeV is inside the old range). 3.8×10⁻¹⁶ is valine's preferred-conformation value and was run as a middle top. Without the factor 2 the values are half these and inside the old range (E72). Wording only: "published amino-acid range" → "range sampled" in the Figure 1 and Figure 3 captions (figures not redrawn; they show the sampled range), Section 3.1 (also "every published value" → "every value sampled", twice) and the Section 6 bullet.
78. **Sensitivity of the shares to the wider top (2026-10-13; `g_wide.py`, `g_wide.log`; predictions committed first in 9aa7353, code-only fix fcecf7b).** New sentences in Section 2.1 and in "Assumed rules and the bias", and one clause in the abstract. g is sampled, so the shares depend on the top. `multiwall.run4` was rerun at the old top (2.64×10⁻¹⁶), 3.8×10⁻¹⁶ and 4.8×10⁻¹⁶ (N = 200,000 per world, seeds 0–3, seed-collision wrapper as in `seedfix.py`). Rise in the PVED-favoured share at 4.8×10⁻¹⁶: 0.0 (early Earth) to 1.2 points (Umbriel), mean 0.7 (`g_wide.log:120–136`). Ranges, old → new (`g_wide.log:138–153`): Ganymede 33.5–44.1 → 33.7–44.5; Europa 22.0–30.1 → 22.2–30.4 (no lid 29.0–39.6 → 29.3–40.1); Enceladus with lid 0.0 → 0.0 (no lid 19.4–26.4 → 19.9–27.1); early Earth 99.7–99.9 unchanged; Umbriel 43.6–53.7 → 44.5–54.8. The old-top rerun reproduces all 150 stored values (`g_wide.log:16`). **The headline numbers in the abstract, Table 3 and Section 3.3 are not replaced:** they come from the full original runs, which were not repeated. The "at least 67%" for early Earth is from `sweep3.py`, which was also run at the wide top: 67.0 → 67.0 (`g_wide.log:20, 88`); the 89% floor of the deepest-hand test (`handoff.py`, `deephand_scale.py`) was not rerun. Why so small: the one-hand mask and the lid do not depend on g, so P_none is exactly constant (0.000 change) and the wider top only moves draws from "chance set the hand" to "PVED set the hand".

The coarsening term (L/A)²/D_h is multiplied by 4 and 10 in every draw (A = 5.5/√f, read by `uncertainty.py`, `sweep2.py`, `sweep3.py` and `multiwall.py`). The margin diagnostic is not in the log, so the paper does not quote it. Code table row added. H14: the factor-10 case covers the measured median slowdown only at a molecular share of 1/8; at 1/32 to 1/4096 the median is 10 to 29 times Eq. 17's (`h14f_advect.log:55-63`), and its growth with the ratio slowed from a power of 0.40 to 0.23–0.24 and then 0.17 over the four doublings to 1/4096 (`:70-73`). The slowdown at the real ratio, about 10⁹, is bracketed only by extrapolation, 29 to 276 (`:90-96`); see E112 and E120.

89. **What a plume would show (2026-10-16; same email; `plume_signal.py`, `plume_signal.log`; predictions committed first in 00ae888).** Section 3.6: the E87 sentence narrowed (the ocean as a whole never has one PVED-set hand with a lid; a plume can be racemic when the water below is not), and a plume paragraph added (layers opposite in 6.5–11% of gate-passing draws for lid-layer shares 0.3–0.01; neither layer amplifies in 21.3% of all draws; alanine survival 0.945 at 10⁵ yr and 0.148 at 10⁷ yr; needed network share 0.0532 for 5% ee and 0.0106 for 1%; P(plume ee ≥ 5%/1%) over all draws at 10⁵ yr 0.231/0.360; PVED-hand share among detectable plumes 0.943). Section 2.7 tally: 7 of 12. Five predictions failed (P3, P4, P6, P10, P11): the layers agree more often than guessed because most gate-passing draws have a large Δ. The independent-layer assumption is stated in the text because it is what lets a lid-layer plume carry the PVED hand while the whole-ocean share stays 0%. Mora et al. (2022) and Steel et al. (2017) were read as abstracts only; no detection limit is attributed to them. Code table row added.
90. **Network share from data (2026-10-16; `plume_share.py`, `plume_share.log`; predictions committed first in b7639a2).** Replaces the guessed network-share prior of E89 as a second estimate, not a replacement of its numbers. Share f = p/(p + B) with p the model's product concentration and B log-uniform 1.00×10⁻⁸ to 1.356×10⁻⁷ M (Steel et al., 2017: 0.01 µM floor; ceiling derived from their 87 g/s maximum abiotic production, 75 g/mol, one amino acid, 10⁵ yr, 2.7×10¹⁹ L — my derivation, not their number). Median f 0.161; f < 0.01 in 31.4%, f > 0.5 in 38.7% of gate-passing draws. P(plume ee ≥ 1/5%) over all draws 0.522/0.449 (was 0.360/0.231); fixed B 10⁻⁹…10⁻⁵ M gives 0.596…0.156 at 5%. 8 of 9 held (P8, the 10⁷-yr range, failed). Risk stated in the text: the rise comes from the assumed feedstock range c (10⁻¹²–10⁻⁴ M, `uncertainty.py`), not from a measurement. Steel's DOI confirmed as 10.1089/ast.2017.1673 (already in the list).
91. **Telling an amplifier from life (2026-10-16; Davila's follow-up; `abiotic_vs_life.py`, `abiotic_vs_life.log`; predictions committed first in c842151).** 10 of 10 held. Source purity ≥ 0.99 in 65.9% of gate-passing draws; Asp racemic while Ala pure in 6.6%; ≥5% ee in all of four amino acids 0.294 with a common share, 0.0093 with independent shares. The plume_signal.log numbers were seen before the predictions (disclosed in the docstring). Literature side (abstracts and the PLoS page only): McKay 2004 and Dorn et al. 2011 added to the reference list; Glavin and Dworkin 2009 already listed. Davila and McKay (2014) not cited: its abstract does not discuss ee or abundance patterns. AIB is achiral and cannot carry an ee.

"Unsettled" kept; Section 4 bias bullet given the reason, citing three papers already in the list. No number changed.

103. **Fluctuation-controlled selection at fixed conditions, Silva-Dias and Weber 2026 (2026-10-17; arXiv:2610.13523 v1 read in full; `journals/silva_dias_weber_2026_note.md`).** One sentence after Eq. 12 in Section 2.2.4 and a reference entry. They take the Kondepudi–Nelson network from master equation to Fokker–Planck, adiabatically eliminate the total density and solve for the splitting probability; at large N it is 1/2 + (cN/π)^½ ψ₀ (their Eq. 32), the first term of Φ((2c)^½ ψ₀ √N) with c a ratio of network constants and ψ₀ the initial excess. Their parameters are fixed past the bifurcation, with no sweep and no persistent rate bias, so nothing in it corresponds to the π^¼ or the (kτ)^¼ of Eq. 11; Eq. 8 with a sudden quench (λ constant, g = 0) gives Δ = α₀(2λ/ε)^½, which is their limit. Their γ is a decay ratio, not the sweep rate. Their ψ diffusion counts production, decay and autocatalysis as Eq. 5 does, and antagonism drops out; the analog of frank.py's F at their Fig. 7 parameters is 0.65, tending to 1/√2 when production is negligible and decay equals copying, so nothing in it contradicts F ≈ 0.7 (README Part 7; the 0.65 is computed in the note, not taken from the paper). The DOI resolves; title, authors and v1 date match the arXiv abstract page. List now 141 entries, 140 with DOIs. No number changed.

Essex et al. 2026 is a ChemRxiv preprint, cited from its abstract only. Scorecard R5 and R9 for both rows changed from ? to N (scores unchanged at 5.0, ranking unchanged). Code availability: Section 5 row adds the two scripts. 106. **Planned experiment: measured r for arm G (2026-10-17; `arm_g_rates.py`, `EXPERIMENT.md`).** Section 5: the statement that Essex et al. 2026 point to r < 1 for arm G replaced by their numbers (r is the rate an L nucleotide adds to a D-RNA primer over the rate a D nucleotide adds, D template, 50/50 pool; the racemic state can be lost only if 1 < r < 1.9): 0.059 for monomer alone (Fig. 1), 0.40 (95% interval 0.22–0.98) for the bridged pathway from k/K and formation rates (Figs. 4, S3), 0.50 for monomer with a D-trimer (Fig. 5), and from the direct competition (Fig. 9) 0.49 if the reduction counts products past +2 or 1.14 (per point 1.06–1.23) if past +1, which the preprint does not say. Model: every r ≤ 0.98 leaves 50/50 stable at every feed (seed-following only; a +20% feed plateaus at 0.67 at r = 0.40); r = 1.14 gives a threshold feed of 10.1 mM. Added that arms E and G are expected to show seed-following only except for the high Fig. 9 reading, and that the hand of the +1 nucleotide in a racemic competition decides between the readings. Essex et al. 2026 is still cited as a preprint; Fig. 1, 4, 5, 9, S3 and S6 numbers are from its main text and SI as read for `arm_g_rates.py`. No reference added, no number in Sections 2–4 changed.

Added that a shared achiral activator with exponential or parabolic template growth never loses the racemic state at r = 0.4–0.6 (0 of 36 cases), and only the hyperbolic control does (6 of 18), on a branch a feed-filled reactor does not reach. The statement that the +1 hand decides between the readings now says the gel fit favours r < 1 and the direct measurement still decides. No reference added, no number in Sections 2–4 changed.

109. **APED peptides via NCAs: scorecard R9 = P (2026-10-19; `aped.py`, `aped.log`, committed in 4b9ee33 with the predictions written before the run; `scorecard.py`, README Part 6).** Section 5: one sentence added after the slow tip-over remark, that a re-implementation of the APED model in its reversible form with monomer racemization (Plasson and Bersini, 2009) loses the racemic state only between 0.0105 and 2.11 M activation fuel, only with Plasson's rate signs (5 of 16 sets; 0 of 16 heterochiral-favouring) and only at about 1 M or more of total residues, so R9 = P; R5 stays ? (NCA activation rate not measured; at the one quoted 10⁻⁸ /s the pond fails, lake and ocean pass). Source: the authors' arXiv versions (Plasson and Bersini 2009, arXiv:0804.4834 v3; arXiv:0804.3939). Corrected 2026-10-20: this entry and the Section 5 sentence first attributed the model to Plasson et al. (2004) and said the PNAS full text could not be read; it is open on PMC534711, and `aped.py` implements the 2009 model, not the 2004 one. A blinded re-implementation of the 2004 model from that full text (`audit/aped_audit.py`, `audit/aped_audit.log`, predictions hashed before the run; Jacobian equal to the paper's Methods matrix M1 to 3.6e-15; Fig. 5 ee at 1 M 0.6949) has no racemization and no lower drive threshold: at the Fig. 5 rates and 1 M of residues the racemic state is lost for every activation rate below 9.96e-7 /s, and at the published rate (1e-8 /s) for total residues above 66.9 mM. Adding racemization (kR = 1e-4 /s) to it creates the lower edge (at 1.82e-3 /s), so the drive threshold behind R9 = P comes from the 2009 racemization step. Section 5 now cites Plasson and Bersini (2009) for the model and states this contrast. Pre-registered predictions: 8 of 11 held; the registered reading of the activation law (rate independent of fuel concentration) failed in run 1 (no upper threshold) and was replaced by the mass-action law, so the thresholds holding is not an independent test of that reading. Reference added: Plasson, Bersini and Commeyras 2004 (PNAS 101 16733–16738; Crossref title, authors, volume and pages match); on 2026-10-20 also Plasson and Bersini 2009 (J. Phys. Chem. B 113 3477–3490, doi:10.1021/jp803807p; Crossref title, authors, volume and pages match). List now 150 entries, 149 with DOIs. No number in Sections 2–4 changed.
110. **Second blind test of the number of deciding units, Jang et al. 2024 (2026-10-20; `blind_jang.py`, `blind_jang.log`, 5 of 7, predictions first in 9de47f0, results in d9b6e47).** New bullet in Section 3.7 after the `blind_units.py` bullet; its opening sentence now names five pre-registered checks, and `blind_jang.py` added to the Section 3.7 row of Code availability; no old number changed. Predictions J1–J5 (sodium chlorate droplets crystallized by an antisolvent at three supersaturations) were hashed (sha256 bd2f04e3…, 2026-10-20 17:49 PDT) and committed after a scout reported the setup only; the predictions file lists what the predictor saw, including the scout's note that the paper reports a critical size. A separate reader took the outcomes from 600 dpi renders of Figs. 3 and 5: mixed droplets from the L&D markers (39, 32 and 27 resolved; 4 and 2 placed by fit inside overlapped clusters to reach the printed totals 43, 32 and 29), L-only and D-only droplets from the histogram bars at bin centres, because the marker rows overlap, as the registered data rule allows. Reader's checks: the entered counts reproduce the paper's printed γ (0.042, 0.058, 0.012), its mixed counts and its droplet totals (379, 618, 276). Held: J1a and J1b (mixed droplets larger, p = 2.5×10⁻⁸ and 0.013), J1c only marginally (p = 0.048; 0.092 without the 2 placed droplets, 0.15 if they sit at 275–350 µm), J3 (559 L, 610 D, p = 0.14), J4 (p = 0.79). Failed: J5 (V0 = 380, 1,150 and 477 nL at 35, 40 and 45 °C, not decreasing). Not scorable: J2 (no size bin reached a single-hand share of 0.4; lowest 0.53). Post hoc, not counted: the fitted exponentials expect 0.3–1.0 mixed droplets below the paper's critical diameters, so finding none does not favour a sharp threshold. Reference added: Jang, Coquerel, Seo, Kim and Park 2024 (Lab Chip 24 5055–5064); Crossref title, authors, volume and pages match. List now 151 entries, 150 with DOIs. `run_all.sh`: `blind_jang.py` added to the fast tier (1 s).
111. **Joyce et al. 1984 read: arm G addition rate (2026-10-21; `joyce_r5.py`, `joyce_r5.log`, 5 of 6, predictions hashed before reading in `joyce_r5_predictions.txt`; `scorecard.py` R5 ? → Y, README Part 6).** Section 5, arm G: one sentence added after the Essex et al. sentence, from Joyce et al.'s Table 1 (poly(C) template, 0 °C, 7 days, 10–100 mM): k₂ = −ln(1−f)/(cT) ≥ 1.7×10⁻⁵ M⁻¹ s⁻¹ all-D and ≥ 1.7×10⁻⁶ racemic. Lower bounds, since f counts only oligomers of length ≥ 4; the racemic value uses total concentration, which the paper does not state (per enantiomer would halve it). Both are above the ocean value (3×10⁻⁹); all-D is above the pond value (3×10⁻⁶ at 1 mM) and racemic below it. `scorecard.py` judges R5 at its own pond setting (10 mM, floor 3×10⁻⁷), which both clear. The missed prediction (P2) was the concentration range: one run was at 0.01 M. Reference already in the list; no other number changed.

113. **R6 seed rule: arms E and G R6 = N (2026-10-21; `scorecard.py`, `inherit.py`, `arm_e_r6.py`, `arm_g_r6.py`, `rna_seed_scan.py`, `aped_r5.py`, README Part 6; `journals/no_lab_run_2026-10-21.md` items 1–5).** `scorecard.py` R6 now uses the `inherit.py` rule: an excess seeded into the pool at the transition is followed (Δ ≥ 2) down to the needed size. Passing a feed excess through does not count, since a stable racemic state passes Δ ≥ 2 that way at a catalysed share of 1.7×10⁻¹⁷ (`arm_e_r6.log:22-24`). Arm E R6 ? → N (a seeded excess is followed only down to 44 times the need, `arm_e_r6.log:67`); arm G R6 ? → N (a template excess is lost in 54 of 54 cases, `arm_g_r6.log:46`); the all-Y feed letters of `rna_seed_scan.py` (`rna_seed_scan.log:115`) noted in the scorecard comment as a counting limit; the APED R5 comment given the activation-rate bracket 1.7×10⁻⁷ to 1.9×10⁻⁵ s⁻¹ (R5 stays ?). Scores and ranking unchanged (? and N both count 0). Section 5: arm G 'seed-following only' → 'only to pass on an excess in its feed'; 'Arm E follows weakly' → 'Arm E does little better'; 'at most 2.4' → 2.45 (2.449, `arm_e_r6.log:55`); one sentence added that neither arm meets the seed rule. README Part 6 changed to match. The literature R6 letters were set under the earlier wording ('works from a racemic or tiny excess') and are not yet re-checked.
114. **R6 seed rule: the eight literature rows R6 = N (2026-10-21; `lit_r6.py`, `lit_r6.log`, `lit_r6_predictions.txt`, hashed before any evidence was gathered (sha256 f0345e50…); `journals/new_analyses_lit_r6.md`; `scorecard.py`, README Part 6).** Under the E113 rule, a hand decided among N_d units cannot follow a seed below about 2/√N_d, so following e_need takes N_d ≥ 1.2×10²³ (pond), 3.8×10²⁷ (lake) and 5.1×10³⁵ (ocean). R6 Y → N for Ghadiri, Soai, Viedma, attrition and magnetite; P → N for the eutectic and APED; Joyce stays N. Grounds: crystals or a surface decide (Viedma, attrition, eutectic, magnetite), the Soai reaction is decided by at most 3.6×10⁹ molecules (`soai_check.py`), no Ghadiri run follows a seed or amplifies ee (Saghatelian et al., 2001), on magnetite the field-set magnetization sets the hand (Ozturk et al., 2023b), and in `aped.py` the racemic state is stable at 0.1, 10⁻², 10⁻⁴ and 10⁻⁶ M (`lit_r6.log:4-8`), so a seed decays. Predictions: 7 of 8 held; F1 failed on one clause (Saghatelian et al. state no growth order). Scores: Ghadiri 6.5 → 5.5, now tied with Joyce for first (the `scorecard.py` assert now checks the top two, not the order between them); Soai 4.5 → 3.5, attrition 6.0 → 5.0, Viedma 5.0 → 4.0, magnetite 4.5 → 3.5, APED 5.0 → 4.5, eutectic 3.0 → 2.5. Order otherwise unchanged; every crystal or surface route still ranks below every solution route. Section 5: one passage added after the seed-rule sentence, saying that no published amplifier meets the rule. The draft has no scorecard table, so the tie appears only in README Part 6. No new reference; no number in Sections 2–4 changed. Rule L, a lenient reading (P where a seed of ≤ 1% grows in the lab or a mechanism model), was offered and not adopted. The re-check's four side findings are fixed in E115.

(2001); its main text gives no growth order, so the clause is removed. R9 = N stands, since it rests only on own-hand catalysis without cross-inhibition, which the paper states. (2) Ozturk et al. (2023b): about 60% ee is the best run, and repeats average about 35%, so Section 1.1, README and `scorecard.py` now say both; Section 1.1 also now says that the second crystallization reaches 100% from about 25% ee or more, as the paper states. (2008, *JACS* 130:1158) went to the R solid, which the authors put down to a trace chiral impurity, and that 0.1 mol% of phenylglycine sets the hand (read in Noorduin's 2010 thesis, which reprints the paper); the reference is added to README. (2006) SI uses proline in DMSO or water (DMF also listed), so it now says that; the paywalled article body was not read. No letter, score or rank changed; no reference was added to the draft.

120. **Eddy slowdown at a molecular share of 1/4096 (2026-10-21; `h14f_advect.py`, `h14f_advect.log`; predictions `h14f_predictions.txt` committed first in cc5f0bb; `journals/new_analyses_h14f_advect.md`).** One cell of 48 runs at 10 wall widths and a molecular share of 1/4096, four doublings past 1/256. Median clearing time 28.85 times Eq. 17's (95% 25.26–29.69; `h14f_advect.log:63`). The exponent over 1/256 to 1/4096 is 0.17 (0.12–0.18), below the 0.19 threshold, so the pre-registered label is FALLING (`:109`); the change from the doubling before is −0.07 (−0.15 to −0.02; `:73`). The six-point logarithmic law predicted 27.88, inside the measured range (`:111`). Second label NOT BELOW (`:110`): the measured exponent, if held to 10⁹, would still pass the early-Earth edge. All eight predictions held (`:99-108`). The kick was rewritten in numba to fit memory and is bitwise equal to the `h14b_advect.py` kick (`:3`). Item 14, the Section 2.3 eddy paragraph, the Section 4 eddies bullet (including its opening range, 5.8 to 18 times now 5.8 to 29), the end of E86 and README Part 6 updated: the extrapolated slowdown at 10⁹ is now 29 (no further growth), 75 (logarithmic) and 131–276 (power law with the measured exponent), 490 to 4.7 × 10³ with the sphere factor (was 300 to 2.7 × 10⁴); early Earth's 99.9% holds under the logarithmic law and is not settled for a power law that holds the measured exponent. The real ratio was not reached.

## G. PAPER_DRAFT.md content deliberately left out

- The novelty claim ("No one had applied this…", the 435-paper scan). No "first" claims are made.
- The candidate amplifiers RAO and Pro-Val-Val; the "three model breaking points"; and the stress-test results (129 s, 10⁻²⁰², quiescent micro-environments). PAPER labels all of these untested.
- The "Multi-Plume Handedness" prediction. It relies on SUDA, which PAPER §3.10 says cannot measure handedness.
- §3.6 (amino-acid/sugar coupling); Parts 1–2, 4 and 4b; the scorecard detail; the beta-decay bias; and the R7 run. All were cut for length.
- Script names and "Part" labels. They appear only in the code table.
- objections.log. Its results are not in PAPER or RNAAS.

On a sphere (`h13_sphere.py`) 663 of 664 random patchworks reached one hand within 30 sweep times and the last at 36 (`h13_sphere_score.log:1`, `h13_sphere_late136.log:3`), and a thin 3D shell 12 of 12 (`h13_sphere_shell.log:14`), so f = 1 stands. Open: the time. The sweep's sideways term is too short by a median factor of 7 (90th percentile 17; `h13_sphere_s16_f50.log:302`); with the factor set to 17 no share moves by more than 0.16 points (`h13_sphere_shares.log:38`). Combined with the eddy slowdown, see item 14 and Section 4.
14. **[Partly resolved by E84, E112 and E120; measured to 30 wall widths and to a molecular share of 1/4096 by `h14c_advect.py`, `h14d_advect.py`, `h14e_advect.py` and `h14f_advect.py`; not closed.]** **Eddy scale separation not shown.** Treating eddies as diffusion needs wall width √(D/k) > eddy size; in 2D, at 10 wall widths, the median wall-removal time was 5.8, 7.7, 10.3, 13.1, 15.3, 18.1 and 28.9 times Eq. 17's time at molecular shares of 1/8, 1/16, 1/32, 1/64, 1/128, 1/256 and 1/4096 (`h14f_advect.log:51-64`). The exponent of the growth with the ratio of total to molecular diffusivity fell from 0.40 over 1/8 to 1/64 (`h14e_advect.log:70`) to 0.23 and 0.24 over the next two doublings and 0.17 (95% range 0.12 to 0.18) over 1/256 to 1/4096, a fall of 0.07 (0.02 to 0.15) from the doubling before (`h14f_advect.log:70-73`). A logarithmic law fitted to the first six points before that run predicted 27.9 at 1/4096, inside the measured range of 25.3 to 29.7 (`:77`, `:111`). Open: the slowdown at the real ratio, about 10⁹, which is still bracketed only by extrapolation over 18 more doublings: 29 if growth stops at 1/4096, 75 for the logarithmic law fitted to all seven points and 131 to 276 if a power law continues with the exponent measured over the last four doublings (`:90-96`). Times the sphere factor of 17 (an assumed product) that is about 490 to 4.7 × 10³; early Earth keeps 99.9% up to about 2600 (Section 4). Not tested: shares below 1/32 at 30 wall widths, 3D, a sphere with eddies, more than one eddy per box.

Sections 1.1 and 4 now credit them only with the abstract's ~15,000-year time scale and argue no volume; the lake-sized body over ~10⁴–10⁵ years is attributed to Quack et al. (2022). No volume is quoted.

# How large a mixed volume does the weak force need? Parity violation and homochirality in Solar System ocean worlds

**Saptarshi Ghosh**
Cupertino High School, [ADD FULL POSTAL ADDRESS], Cupertino, California, USA

**Corresponding author:** Saptarshi Ghosh, [ADD POSTAL ADDRESS], [ADD TELEPHONE], [ADD E-MAIL]. ORCID: [ADD IF AVAILABLE]

## Abstract

Life uses left-handed amino acids and right-handed sugars. The weak nuclear force makes mirror-image molecules differ slightly in energy (the parity-violating energy difference, PVED). In the Kondepudi–Nelson model this bias acts on a slow self-amplifying reaction; I ask when it, not chance, sets the outcome. I use a stochastic normal form of Frank-type self-amplification, checked against simulations and a reaction network. Over the amino-acid PVEDs sampled (0.1–6.2 aeV; the widest published value, 11.4 aeV, moves no percentage below by over 1.2 points), at least 5×10³⁰ to 2×10³⁴ molecules must react as one well-mixed pool. Ponds fall short at every PVED; a mixed lake suffices only at the largest. I therefore test ocean worlds, varying all uncertain inputs, including racemization and vertical mixing that strengthens with depth. Weak vertical mixing freezes an ocean into stacked layers of opposite hand; the walls rise to the ice, and the deepest layer's hand fills the ocean. The PVED-favoured hand fills it in at least 67% of plausible input sets for early Earth (89–99.9% if this deepest-layer rule decides multi-wall sets, tested to walls 1/400 of the depth), 34–44% for Ganymede, 22–30% for Europa and 0% for Enceladus, where a stratified layer under thin ice traps the wall (19–26% without it). In every ocean, chance sets the hand in at most 15% of input sets; most single-hand outcomes follow the PVED. A closed reversible network amplifies an excess, then loses it; a driven network holds it. Asteroid Bennu's racemic amino acids fit this if the parent body's drive stopped while water remained. A lasting excess in a driven ocean, such as Enceladus's, would test amplification; only a consistent sign across independent oceans or molecule classes would test the PVED. No known prebiotic reaction amplifies this way; a wet-lab search is planned. All code is public.

## 1 Introduction

Life uses left-handed (L) amino acids in proteins and right-handed (D) sugars in DNA and RNA. Each mirror-image form is chemically identical to the other when alone. Why life uses one hand, and why this one, is still open.

### 1.1 Previous proposals that the weak force selected biological handedness

The idea that the weak force set the hand of life is almost as old as the discovery of parity violation (1956–1957; Quack et al., 2022).

**An amplifier before a bias (1953).** Frank (1953) proposed that autocatalysis plus mutual inhibition lets small random fluctuations tip a racemic mixture almost entirely into one enantiomer (as summarised in the abstract of Soai et al., 1995). The outcome is set by chance; a systematic push was missing.

**Parity violation as the push (1959–1968).** Ulbricht (1959) reviewed parity non-conservation together with optical activity. Vester, Ulbricht and Krauch (1959) and Ulbricht and Vester (1962) proposed and tested a beta-decay route: longitudinally polarized beta electrons slow down in matter and emit circularly polarized bremsstrahlung, which could act asymmetrically on organic molecules. Across about ten reactions with several beta emitters they found no optical activity above 0.02% (Bonner, 1974). Yamagata (1966) put forward a hypothesis for the asymmetric appearance of biomolecules on Earth; later work calls the link between electroweak energy differences and homochirality "the Yamagata hypothesis" (Wesendrup et al., 2003), and Rein (1974) described it as an accumulation mechanism acting over geological time. Garay (1968) bombarded D- and L-tyrosine with beta-particles and found that the D isomer decomposed more quickly.

**Two separate weak-force routes (1974–1982).** The field then split: chiral radiation from beta-decay acts at a particular place and time, while the parity-violating energy difference (PVED) between enantiomers comes from weak neutral currents inside each molecule. Rein (1974) showed that the weak interaction does distinguish mirror-image molecules, but concluded that within the weak-interaction theory of the time the energy difference would be tiny. Letokhov (1975) proposed measuring it as a small shift between the infrared vibration–rotation lines of the two enantiomers (Quack et al., 2022). Hegstrom, Rein and Sandars (1980) developed the theory of these energy shifts. Their calculations for twisted ethylene and A-nor-2-thiacholestane gave shifts appreciably smaller than earlier estimates. On the beta-decay route, Hegstrom (1982) found a mechanism by which longitudinally polarized electrons ionize a chiral molecule and its mirror image at different rates. In companion experiments, Gidley et al. (1982) used a spin-polarized positron beam and found no asymmetry in cystine or tryptophan at the 7 × 10⁻⁴ level, but a possible effect of (31 ± 7) × 10⁻⁴ in leucine.

**Ab initio PVEDs for biomolecules (1983–1985).** Ab initio PVED calculations (Mason and Tranter, 1983, 1984) gave L-alanine in its aqueous conformation, and L-peptides in the alpha-helix and beta-sheet, a lower ground-state energy than the D forms. The difference was of order 10⁻¹⁴ J mol⁻¹. That corresponds to an excess of about 10⁶ L molecules per mole of racemate at equilibrium at ambient temperature (Mason and Tranter, 1985). Tranter (1985a) extended the calculations to alpha-amino acids. Mason (1987) contrasted this constant, uniform bias, which lowers the energy of L-amino acids and of the parent aldotriose of the D-sugar series, with chiral fields and beta-decay agents that need a particular time or place, and named two ways it could matter: as a trigger that guides an autocatalytic racemic process into one channel, or through cumulative amplification in a large homochiral aggregate. Tranter (1985b) stated the cost of the first: the differences are so small that propagating homochirality would need an amplifier with both large quantities of reactants and a long reaction time.

**Bias at a bifurcation: Kondepudi and Nelson (1983–1987).** This is the work my model builds on most directly. Kondepudi and Nelson (1983, 1984a) analysed chiral symmetry breaking in nonequilibrium chemical systems and the sensitivity of the symmetry-breaking states, and Kondepudi and Nelson (1984b) derived time scales for chiral selection. In systems that evolve to an L- or D-dominated state, a systematic chiral interaction no stronger than the weak-neutral-current energy in amino acids can determine which enantiomer dominates over about 15,000 years (Kondepudi and Nelson, 1985), in line with Mason and Tranter's finding for L-amino acids. Kondepudi (1987) gave the general form: slow passage through the transition point makes the system sensitive to very weak but systematic chiral influences. Later accounts place their estimate in a lake-sized body of water over about 10⁴–10⁵ years (Quack et al., 2022).

**Doubts about the size and the sign (1991–2003).** Bonner (1991) reviewed the origin and amplification of biomolecular chirality, including the beta-decay and PVED proposals. For sugars the lower-energy enantiomer depends on conformation: D with a C2-endo furanose pucker, L with a C3-endo pucker (Tranter et al., 1992). MacDermott (1995) reported PVEDs for a range of important biomolecules, with the natural enantiomer the more stable in nearly all cases. Zanasi et al. (1999) computed the parity-violating energy shift of five L-amino acids, of hydrated D-glyceraldehyde (the simplest chiral sugar) and of D-camphor, using non-relativistic coupled-method approximations. For the amino acids the shift changed sign with the dihedral angle, and the largest negative value fell at the conformation preferred in water; for glyceraldehyde the best calculation was negative at every angle tried. The authors reported shifts about ten times larger than any earlier ones. Keszthelyi (1999), in a short review of the preceding few years, gave the amino-acid value as about 10⁻¹⁷ kT, with the L form lower, noted that newer calculations raise it by one to two orders of magnitude, and still called it questionable that so small a bias could push a developing system in one direction. He put the lack of convincing experiments down to the weakness of the effect in carbon-centred molecules.

Improved electroweak quantum chemistry changed the numbers: Berger and Quack (2000) concluded that numerous earlier claims of L-alanine stabilization were unjustified. Laerdahl, Wesendrup and Schwerdtfeger (2000) found L-alanine lower in energy for only seven of 13 gas-phase conformers, which allowed no definite conclusion. Wesendrup et al. (2003) tested the Yamagata hypothesis with relativistic calculations of the parity-violating energy for amino acids and found no clear evidence that parity violation stabilizes the natural L-amino acids. Quack (2002) reviewed the field and noted that the newer methods give PVEDs one to two orders of magnitude larger than previously accepted, but still very small (femtojoule to picojoule per mole).

**Doubts about the mechanism (1996–2007).** Bonner (1996) found no experimental evidence that beta-decay electrons or other spin-polarized particles generate chiral molecules, only theoretical support for a causal link between PVEDs and homochirality, and no bias in his own crystallization tests with very efficient stereoselective autocatalysis. Bonner (2000) went further and concluded that nuclear-level and biopolymer-level parity violation are entirely independent. Avetisov (1999) raised a different kind of objection from the kinetics. A weak chiral field can purify a pool of monomers if its advantage is large compared with the rate of racemization, but he argued that a medium made pure this way could not stay pure as enantiospecific biochemical functions arose, and that purity from spontaneous symmetry breaking among simple molecules needs catalysts about as selective as enzymes. He concluded that chiral physical factors could not be of significant consequence for the origin of biological homochirality, while stating that this does not rule out an asymmetric origin of life. Treating the problem stochastically (Lente, 2004), Lente found that the chance of the more stable isomer being in excess is not significantly above 50% under normal conditions (Lente, 2006), and that enantioselective autocatalysis cannot amplify the PVED under conditions reasonable for the origin of biological chirality (Lente, 2007).

**No detection yet.** The PVED has still not been detected experimentally. Szabó-Nagy and Keszthelyi (1999) reported a possible sign of it: crystals grown from racemic solutions of cobalt and iridium complexes (metals chosen because the effect grows steeply with atomic number) were on average slightly richer in one hand, which the authors read as support for the PVED. I treat this as an unconfirmed claim, not a detection. The signals were close to the sensitivity limit of the instrument, the size of the effect depended on a crystal-growth exponent that the authors could only infer by assuming a theoretical PVED, and the chapter's own introduction says that experimental confirmation was still missing. Current calculations give about 10⁻¹¹ to 10⁻¹⁰ J mol⁻¹ for heavier chiral molecules (Quack et al., 2022), but much less for the amino acids: about 10⁻¹⁴ J mol⁻¹ (0.1 aeV) in the first ab initio work (Mason and Tranter, 1985), about ten times more in second-generation calculations (MacDermott et al., 2009), and 6×10⁻¹³ J mol⁻¹ (6.2 aeV) for alanine as tabulated by Quack et al. (2022).

**Amplifiers that work in the laboratory (1995–2007).** Soai et al. (1995) showed that an autocatalytic organozinc reaction raises a 2% enantiomeric excess of a pyrimidyl alkanol. Blackmond (2004) explained this: the product catalyses its own formation and suppresses that of its enantiomer, as in Frank's model. Viedma (2005) showed that grinding-driven crystallization with recycling turns a symmetric population of D and L crystals into one of complete chiral purity. Plasson et al. (2007) classified these far-from-equilibrium mechanisms and stressed recycling and network autocatalysis. They supply the amplifier the Kondepudi–Nelson argument assumes, but none is the aqueous prebiotic reaction it needs (Section 4).

**Space, planets and noise (1999–2023).** Lunine et al. (1999) proposed Titan, with its large area, great age, organic-rich chemistry and no life, as a natural reactor far larger and longer-lived than any laboratory, in which to ask whether abiotic chemistry evolves toward enantiomeric purity. They noted that racemization rates at Titan's surface temperatures are unknown and called for chiral analysis in situ. Brandenburg and Multamäki (2004) solved reaction-diffusion equations for left- and right-handed populations on Earth's surface. They found that global homochirality is set by the long global diffusion time, which turbulence and large-scale flows can shorten, and suggested that a quiescent deep ocean could have let opposite hands coexist. Gleiser (2007) used a biased model with cross-inhibition to follow the growth of left- and right-handed domains in space. He bounded the chiral bias from constraints on the emergence of life on early Earth and discussed weak neutral currents and circularly polarized light as sources; he noted that the approach applies to any planetary setting. Gleiser and Walker (2012) argued that environmental disturbances may have set life's hand and predicted that a representative sample of extraterrestrial stereochemistry will be racemic on average. Hochberg et al. (2022) reported that parity-violation-induced chiral selectivity is resilient to nonequilibrium temperature fluctuations in open systems. Hochberg et al. (2023) framed this as an imperfect bifurcation in which chiral selectivity holds when the bias exceeds the noise.

**Competing deterministic sources.** Circularly polarized infrared light is strong in the Orion OMC-1 star-forming region. Polarized light at shorter wavelengths could bias interstellar organics that were later delivered to Earth (Bailey et al., 1998). Spin-polarized electrons from UV-irradiated magnetite could drive enantioselective reductions through the chiral-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect (Ozturk and Sasselov, 2022). Crystallizing an RNA precursor on magnetite gave about 60% ee in the best run and about 35% on average over repeats, and a second crystallization from about 25% ee or more gave 100% (Ozturk et al., 2023b). Enantiopure crystals can in turn magnetize the surface (Ozturk et al., 2023a). Both are local agents of the kind Mason (1987) contrasted with the uniform weak force. Section 4 shows how the magnetite route enters my early-Earth and icy-moon results.

**What this work adds.** I keep the structure of the Kondepudi–Nelson model: a Frank-type amplifier crosses a symmetry-breaking bifurcation, and the PVED acts as a small systematic bias against random fluctuations. Kondepudi and Nelson (1985) already argued that such a bias needs a large system and a long time (a lake-sized body over about 10⁴–10⁵ years in later accounts; Quack et al., 2022), and the "large quantities of reactants and a long reaction time" of Tranter (1985b) make the same point. I restate that balance as a count of molecules in one connected, well-mixed volume, about 5×10³⁰ to 2×10³⁴ over the published amino-acid PVEDs (Section 3.1), consistent with Lente's (2006, 2007) conclusion that the bias is lost in ordinary settings. Brandenburg and Multamäki (2004) and Gleiser (2007) studied domain growth on a single planet. What is new here is narrower: how anisotropic mixing stretches the mixed volume (Eq. 15), how stratification pins the wall between hands, and how a wall past the pinning onset reverses instead (Eq. 20), and a Monte Carlo over planetary inputs that turns these into a ranking of named worlds. This does not settle the earlier objections: if the PVED is smaller than assumed, or has the opposite sign for the relevant prebiotic molecules (Berger and Quack, 2000; Laerdahl et al., 2000; Tranter et al., 1992), the scale rule and the ranking change with it.

### 1.2 This work

For the amino acids the published PVEDs are about 4×10⁻¹⁸ to 5×10⁻¹⁶ kT at 0 °C (Section 2.1). Whether the PVED or chance set life's hand remains open (Quack et al., 2022). Unlike the few-molecule start of Lente (2007), my model assumes an open, fed reaction in which the whole population passes through the transition together. Cowan and Furnstahl (2022) computed the PVED for an alkaline-earth-ion-catalysed autocatalytic sugar (formose) step and compounded it over turnovers at an early-ocean temperature, without fluctuations. Unlike Lunine et al. (1999), I consider liquid-water oceans only.

Here I ask two linked questions. First, how many molecules must react together for the PVED, rather than chance, to control the outcome? Second, does any real body of water meet that requirement? I use the Solar System's ocean worlds as test beds. The sampling includes racemization, separate feedstock and product concentrations, and vertical mixing that strengthens with depth, and I test in simulations which layer's hand fills a stratified ocean, whether independent patches add up to one pool, and what happens when the transition sweeps across the water as a front. All life on Earth shares one ancestor, so Earth's single hand cannot tell a physical bias from luck. A second, independent ocean could.

## 2 Methods

Figure 1 summarises the model.

![Figure 1. Schematic of the model.](fig_schematic2.png)

**Figure 1.** Schematic of the model (no simulated or measured data). (a) Fixed points of the noise-free normal form, λα − α³ + g = 0 (Eq. 6), against the control parameter λ; solid lines are stable, dashed unstable, dotted grey the symmetric pitchfork (g = 0). The bias is drawn as g = 0.05 so that it is visible (the real bias is about 10⁻¹⁷). Before the transition (λ < 0) the only stable state is offset from racemic by about g/|λ|; after it (λ > 0) the connected branch leads to the PVED-favoured hand. The grey band marks, schematically, the window |λ| ≲ γ^½ in which the outcome is set (Eq. 9). (b) An ice-covered ocean, not to scale: ice shell, a stratified lid that forms when the salinity S is below S* (Section 2.5), and a convecting interior of depth H with horizontal and vertical eddy diffusivities D_h and D_z. A wall of width about √(D/k) separates an L-dominated region (α ≈ +1) from a D-dominated one (α ≈ −1). (c) The scale rule, Eq. 13: the number of molecules N needed for Δ = 2 (a 97.7% probability that the PVED-favoured hand is selected) over the amino-acid range of Table 1 (g = 4.4×10⁻¹⁸ to 2.64×10⁻¹⁶), for kτ = 10 (solid) and 10⁵ (dashed). The dot is the reference case (g = 10⁻¹⁷, kτ = 10; 3.6×10³³ molecules). Horizontal lines are the molecules in a 1 m³ pond at 1 M (6×10²⁶) and in the fully mixed Enceladus (2.7×10¹⁶ m³) and Europa (3.3×10¹⁸ m³) oceans at 1 µM. Drawn by `journals/make_fig_schematic2.py`, which checks these values against `review.py: n_needed()` and `sweep2.log`.

### 2.1 The bias: the parity-violating energy difference

The weak nuclear force does not conserve parity. As a result, the two mirror-image forms (enantiomers) of a chiral molecule are not exactly equal in energy. The difference is the parity-violating energy difference, ΔE_pv. It is set by the weak interaction between electrons and nuclei, and it grows steeply with nuclear charge. Across molecules made of light atoms (H, C, N, O) it spans attoelectronvolts (aeV) to femtoelectronvolts (feV), and with sulfur, chlorine or bromine it can reach about 200 aeV (Quack et al., 2022). For the amino acids, the molecules this paper is about, the published magnitudes sit at the low end of that span: from about 0.1 aeV (10⁻¹⁴ J mol⁻¹; Mason and Tranter, 1983, 1985), through second-generation values about ten times larger (MacDermott et al., 2009), to 6.2 aeV (6.0×10⁻¹³ J mol⁻¹) for alanine (as tabulated by Quack et al., 2022). It has never been measured directly, and its sign for the amino acids in water is not settled (Quack et al., 2022).

I express the bias as the dimensionless ratio of this energy to the thermal energy:

$$ g = \frac{\Delta E_{pv}}{k_B T}. \tag{1}$$

Here k_B is Boltzmann's constant and T is the temperature. In the chemistry of Section 2.2, g enters as a fractional difference between the copying rate constants of the two hands: the L hand copies at k(1 + g) and the D hand at k(1 − g). This follows Kondepudi and Nelson (1985), who proposed that the PVED acts as a small bias on the rates of a self-amplifying reaction.

Strictly, a rate depends on the energy of the transition state relative to the reactant, so k_L/k_D = exp(−[ΔE_pv(TS) − ΔE_pv(R)]/k_BT), where ΔE_pv(TS) and ΔE_pv(R) are the PVEDs of the transition state and of the reactant. Setting g ≈ ΔE_pv/k_BT is therefore an order-of-magnitude assumption: the transition-state difference may be smaller or larger than ΔE_pv, and since N ∝ g⁻² (Eq. 13), a factor r in g changes the required N by r⁻². For flexible molecules the relevant PVED is also a Boltzmann average over conformers, whose individual values can differ in sign (Tranter et al., 1992; Zanasi et al., 1999), so the average can be much smaller than any single value. In the sampling I therefore multiply g by a rate-bias factor η drawn log-uniformly from 0.1 to 10 (an assumed range; I found no published transition-state PVED for an amino acid). The sampled range of ηg (Table 1) covers a smaller or larger magnitude, but not a sign flip or a near-cancellation.

The reference value is g = 10⁻¹⁷, taken at 0 °C (T = 273 K) in `review.py: r1_scale()`, which sets ΔE_pv = 10⁻¹⁷ × R × 273 J/mol. In the uncertainty sampling (Section 2.6), g is drawn log-uniformly from 4.4×10⁻¹⁸ to 2.64×10⁻¹⁶ (`sweep2.py`, step a), which is 0.10 to 6.2 aeV per molecule at 273 K (1 aeV = 9.65×10⁻¹⁴ J mol⁻¹). Zanasi et al. (1999) tabulate larger shifts. Their chapter defines the PVED as twice the tabulated shift, and with that factor their extended-basis values for the conformations preferred in water are 7.2–9.0 aeV (g = 3.1–3.8×10⁻¹⁶) and the largest, for valine at one dihedral angle, is 11.4 aeV (4.8×10⁻¹⁶). The published range is therefore 4.4×10⁻¹⁸ to 4.8×10⁻¹⁶, and the sampling above covers the part of it up to the alanine value of Quack et al. (2022). Raising the top of the sampling to 4.8×10⁻¹⁶ (`g_wide.py`, same seeds and settings) raises the PVED-favoured share of every world by 0.0 to 1.2 points (mean 0.7), leaves early Earth at 99.9% and Enceladus with a lid at 0%, and changes no order (`g_wide.log:120–153`); the headline numbers in this paper are for the range above. The lower end is the smallest published magnitude, not a physical floor: averaging over conformers in water can give less, and the sign is not settled. The reference value g = 10⁻¹⁷ (0.24 aeV) lies near the low end of this range. The base model of the earlier sampling (`uncertainty.py: sweep()`) drew g from 4×10⁻¹⁹ to 4×10⁻¹⁶ and had D_z uniform with depth; results that were not recomputed with the additions of Section 2.6 are labelled "uniform-D_z base model". Because ΔE_pv is a fixed energy, g falls as 1/T; Section 3.1 gives the effect of temperature on the result.

### 2.2 The chemistry and the equation it reduces to

#### 2.2.1 A Frank-type network

A bias of 10⁻¹⁷ has no effect on a single reaction. It can matter only in a reaction that copies its own product, so that a small excess grows, and in which the two hands remove each other, so that only one hand can become dominant (Frank, 1953). I use the following network in a flow reactor (`frank.py: run()`). A is an achiral feedstock (for example an amino-acid precursor), and L and D are the two hands of the product.

| Reaction | Rate (M s⁻¹) | Physical role |
|---|---|---|
| feed → A | f (a₀(t) − a) | supply of achiral feedstock A; a₀ is raised slowly |
| A → L, A → D | k₀ a each | slow, unbiased, uncatalysed production |
| A + L → 2L | k (1 + g) a L | self-copying of L, with the weak-force bias |
| A + D → 2D | k (1 − g) a D | self-copying of D |
| L + D → inactive product | k_i L D | mutual removal (cross-inhibition) |
| L → out, D → out | k_d L, k_d D | outflow |

Symbols and units: a, L and D are the concentrations of A and of the two hands (M = mol/L); a₀ is the feed concentration (M); f is the flow rate (s⁻¹); k₀ is the uncatalysed rate constant (s⁻¹); k is the self-copying rate constant (M⁻¹ s⁻¹; this is k₂ in the ocean model); k_i is the cross-inhibition rate constant (M⁻¹ s⁻¹); k_d is the outflow rate (s⁻¹); g is dimensionless (Eq. 1). The rate equations are

$$\frac{da}{dt} = f\,(a_0 - a) - 2k_0 a - k a\left[(1+g)L + (1-g)D\right], \tag{2a}$$
$$\frac{dL}{dt} = k_0 a + k(1+g)\,aL - k_i LD - k_d L, \tag{2b}$$
$$\frac{dD}{dt} = k_0 a + k(1-g)\,aD - k_i LD - k_d D. \tag{2c}$$

In the simulations each reaction also carries its own counting noise (the chemical Langevin equation): a reaction with rate r adds a random change of standard deviation √(r dt/Ω) to the concentrations it changes in a time step dt, where Ω is the number of molecules per unit concentration in the reactor (molecules per M; Ω = 1000·N_A·V for a volume V in m³). This is the term `n = sqrt(r*dt/omega)*...` in `frank.py: run()`. The simulations use dimensionless constants k = k_d = k_i = 1, k₀ = 10⁻³ and f = 1, with a₀ ramped from 0.8 to 1.6 over a time τ.

A second network (`frank2.py: run()`) adds the reverse reactions L → A, D → A (rate constant 0.2) and L + L → A + L, D + D → A + D (rate constant 0.5). I use it only as a check (Section 2.7).

#### 2.2.2 Reduction near the symmetry-breaking point

Write the sum and the difference of the two hands as s = L + D and u = L − D. Subtracting Eq. 2c from Eq. 2b gives, exactly,

$$\frac{du}{dt} = (ka - k_d)\,u + g\,k a\, s. \tag{3}$$

Cross-inhibition removes one L and one D together, so it does not appear in Eq. 3. The first term says that a difference between the hands grows when copying outpaces outflow (ka > k_d) and decays otherwise. The second term is the bias.

On the racemic branch (u = 0, g = 0), adding Eqs. 2b and 2c and setting the result to zero gives 2k₀a + (ka − k_d)s − (k_i/2)s² = 0. At the crossing point, where ka = k_d, each hand has concentration x = s/2 = √(k₀a/k_i). With the constants above this is x = √(10⁻³) ≈ 0.032, as used in `frank.py: main()`.

Because a₀ is raised slowly, a rises and the growth rate of u, λ_u = ka − k_d, passes through zero at a time t_c. Near t_c it is linear in time:

$$\lambda_u(t) \approx \gamma\,(t - t_c), \qquad \gamma = k\,\frac{da}{dt} \quad (\text{s}^{-2}). \tag{4}$$

The noise on u comes from every reaction that changes L − D by one molecule: the two uncatalysed productions, the two copying steps and the two outflows. Cross-inhibition changes L − D by zero and contributes nothing. At L = D = x the variance of u grows at the rate

$$\varepsilon_u = \frac{2k_0 a + 2x\,(ka + k_d)}{\Omega} \quad (\text{M}^2\,\text{s}^{-1}). \tag{5}$$

Near t_c, therefore, du = [γ(t − t_c)u + g_eff] dt + √ε_u dW, with g_eff = 2g·x·ka. These are the three quantities computed in `frank.py: predicted()`. For frank2.py the same bookkeeping gives λ = ka − k₀r − k_d − 2k_r x and ε = (2k₀a + 2k₀r x + 2kax + 2k_r x² + 2k_d x)/Ω (`frank2.py: mapped()`).

Once |u| is no longer small, feedstock depletion and cross-inhibition limit its growth. Because the equations are unchanged when L and D are exchanged together with g → −g, the leading saturating term in the excess is cubic. Writing α = (L − D)/(L + D), measuring time in units of 1/k and rescaling α so that the cubic coefficient is 1 gives the normal form

$$d\alpha = \left(\lambda(t)\,\alpha - \alpha^3 + g\right)dt + \sqrt{\varepsilon}\,dW, \qquad \lambda = \gamma t. \tag{6}$$

This is implemented in `sim.py: simulate()`. Its symbols are dimensionless: λ measures how far the system is past the transition; g is the bias; ε is the noise strength; γ = 1/(kτ) is the sweep rate, with k = k₂c the copying rate (k₂ in M⁻¹ s⁻¹ times the reactant concentration c in M) and τ the time over which conditions pass through the transition; W is a Wiener process. For λ < 0 the only stable state is near α = 0; for λ > 0 there are two stable states near α = +1 and α = −1 (Figure 1a). I did not derive the cubic coefficient for the network; the result below depends only on the linear stage, and Section 2.7 tests it against the full network directly.

#### 2.2.3 Which state is selected

The outcome is set while α is still small, so the cubic term can be dropped. Equation 6 becomes linear,

$$d\alpha = (\gamma t\,\alpha + g)\,dt + \sqrt{\varepsilon}\,dW, \tag{7}$$

and its solution is

$$\alpha(t) = e^{\gamma t^2/2}\left[\alpha(t_0)\,e^{-\gamma t_0^2/2} + \int_{t_0}^{t} e^{-\gamma s^2/2}\left(g\,ds + \sqrt{\varepsilon}\,dW_s\right)\right]. \tag{8}$$

The first term in the bracket vanishes when the sweep starts well before the transition (t₀ ≪ −γ^(−½)). For t ≫ γ^(−½), the prefactor grows without bound and the bracket tends to a fixed random number Z. The sign of Z sets the final state, because the cubic term only stops α at ±1 and cannot change its sign. Z is Gaussian, with

$$\langle Z\rangle = g\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-\gamma s^2/2}\,ds = g\sqrt{2\pi/\gamma}, \qquad \operatorname{Var} Z = \varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-\gamma s^2}\,ds = \varepsilon\sqrt{\pi/\gamma}. \tag{9}$$

Both integrals are dominated by |t| ≲ γ^(−½), that is |λ| ≲ γ^½: this is the window in which the outcome is set (grey band in Figure 1a). The probability that the PVED-favoured state is selected is P = Pr(Z > 0):

$$P = \Phi(\Delta), \qquad \Delta = \frac{\langle Z\rangle}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var} Z}} = \sqrt{2}\,\pi^{1/4}\,g\,\gamma^{-1/4}\,\varepsilon^{-1/2}, \tag{10}$$

where Φ is the standard normal cumulative distribution (`sim.py: delta()`). Δ = 2 gives P = 97.7%. Physically, Δ compares the excess that the bias builds up during the window with the excess that counting noise builds up in the same window. A slower sweep (smaller γ) widens the window, and both grow, but the bias grows faster, which is why Δ rises as γ^(−¼). Figure 2 shows the same picture as a tilted landscape.

![Figure 2 Sketch of the landscape that the excess α moves in (Eq. 6).](../sketch.png)

**Figure 2.** Sketch of the landscape that the excess α moves in (Eq. 6). The weak force tilts the landscape very slightly, and molecular counting noise shakes it. Which valley α ends in is set in a short window around the transition (Eqs. 9 and 10).

#### 2.2.4 The scale rule

For a pool of N molecules that react together, the counting noise in α is ε ≈ 1/N, and with time in units of 1/k the sweep rate is γ = 1/(kτ). Equation 10 becomes

$$\Delta = \sqrt{2}\,\pi^{1/4}\,g\,(k\tau)^{1/4}\sqrt{N}, \qquad k = k_2 c. \tag{11}$$

For a body of water of volume V (m³), of which a fraction f is mixed together during the window,

$$N = c\,V f \cdot 1000 \cdot N_A, \tag{12}$$

where N_A is Avogadro's number and the factor 1000 converts m³ to litres. Equations 11 and 12 together are `ocean.py: delta_at()`. Silva-Dias and Weber (2026) derived the selection probability for the Kondepudi–Nelson network held at fixed conditions past the bifurcation, with an initial excess as the only bias; their large-system result also grows as √N, with a prefactor set by the network's rate constants and no sweep rate. Equation 8 gives that result for a sudden quench (λ constant, initial excess α₀, g = 0: Δ = α₀(2λ/ε)^½), so Eq. 11 is its slow-sweep, persistent-bias counterpart.

The bias controls the outcome when Δ ≥ 2: the bias accumulated over the window exceeds twice the standard deviation of the noise accumulated over it, the balance that underlies the proposal of Kondepudi and Nelson (1985). Squaring Eq. 11 (Δ² = 2π^½ g² (kτ)^½ N) and setting Δ = 2 gives

$$N \ge \left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{2}\,\pi^{1/4}\,g\,(k\tau)^{1/4}}\right)^2 = \frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}\;g^2\,(k\tau)^{1/2}}. \tag{13}$$

This is `review.py: n_needed()`. The reaction must also run to completion within the available time, which I require as kτ = k₂cτ ≥ 10 (`ocean.py: c_time()`). With g = 10⁻¹⁷ and kτ = 10, Eq. 13 gives N ≥ 3.6×10³³ molecules, or 6×10⁹ mol (`review.py: r1_scale()`). N scales as g⁻² and as (kτ)^(−½) (results in Section 3.1). In the sampling, k is set by the feedstock concentration a and N by the product concentration p (Section 2.6).

The full network adds noise that the simple count ε = 1/N leaves out: uncatalysed production and outflow change L − D as much as copying does (Eq. 5). Dividing Eq. 10 for the network by Eq. 11 for the same molecules gives Δ_network = F·Δ, with F = √(x/(k₀ + 2x)) = 0.70 at the constants of `frank.py` (`frank.py: main()`); the reversible network gives F = 0.71 (`frank2.py: mapped()`). Since Δ ∝ c^(3/4) at fixed V (Eqs. 11, 12), the required concentration rises by F^(−4/3) = 1.60. The uncertainty sampling draws F from U(0.3, 0.7) (Table 1), whose upper end matches these values and whose lower end allows for chemistry with more wasted turnover.

Eq. 13 counts every molecule as an independent vote (ε = 1/N), the least noise a pool of N molecules can have, so its N is a minimum. In each crystallization of Section 3.7 where the deciding units can be counted, far fewer decided the outcome than the vessel held (about 1–300 against 10⁶–10¹²), because copying from a few early nuclei outran mixing. Nuclei need not behave like molecules in solution, but no measurement yet shows that a solution reaction reaches ε = 1/N either. Any noise of this kind beyond F raises the molecules needed above Eq. 13.

### 2.3 Why mixing sets the number of molecules

N in Eq. 11 is not the number of molecules in a body of water. It is the number whose counting noise is averaged together while the outcome is set. Two regions that do not exchange molecules during the window each have their own noise, their own Z in Eq. 9, and so they can end in opposite states. The relevant N is therefore the number of molecules in a volume that is connected and mixed on the time scale of the reaction.

To describe this I let α vary in space. Mixing by ocean eddies acts on large scales like diffusion, with a horizontal eddy diffusivity D_h and a vertical one D_z (both m² s⁻¹). Equation 6 becomes the field equation

$$\partial_t \alpha = \lambda\alpha - \alpha^3 + g + D_h\nabla_h^2\alpha + D_z\,\partial_z^2\alpha + \text{noise}, \tag{14}$$

implemented in 1D in `domains.py: sweep()`, in 3D with one D in `domains3d.py: sweep3d()` and `coarsen3d.py: step()`, with separate D_h and D_z in `aniso3d.py: run()`, and on a spherical shell in `sphere_layers.py`.

Ocean mixing is strongly anisotropic: D_z is many orders of magnitude smaller than D_h (Table 1). Changing the vertical coordinate to z' = z·√(D_h/D_z) turns D_z ∂_z² into D_h ∂_{z'}², so Eq. 14 becomes the equal-mixing equation in a box whose depth is stretched from H to

$$H' = H\sqrt{D_h/D_z}. \tag{15}$$
