Weak vertical mixing therefore acts like a deep, narrow ocean. I take the ocean depth as H = V/(4πR²) with R = L/π, and the horizontal size as half the circumference, L = πR (`uncertainty.py: depth()`). Comparing H' with L sorts every input set into one of two cases:

- H' ≤ L (the stretched ocean is wider than it is deep). After the transition the ocean forms a patchwork of regions of each hand that coarsens to a single domain (Section 2.4), and I count the whole volume: f = 1 in Eq. 12. This is an assumption. Independent patches that coarsen act as one pool with Δ reduced by a factor c of 0.92–0.98 (`kz_patches.py`, `kz_large.py`; Section 3.2), so using f = 1 rather than f = c² overstates Δ by about 2% in 3D; a shallow-slab test (Section 3.2) does not fully support the single domain.
- H' > L (the stretched ocean is a tall column). The patchwork freezes into stacked horizontal layers of opposite hand (Section 2.4). On a sphere the top layer later expands to fill the whole depth, so the outcome is set by the molecules of the top layer alone. I assume that this layer has a thickness of about L in stretched units, so it holds a share f = L/H' of the molecules. This share is an assumption, based on the 3–5 layers seen in `aniso3d.py`; two `sphere_layers.py` runs gave top-layer shares of 0.52 and 0.07 against L/H' = 0.25, which neither supports nor rules it out (`uncertainty.py` docstring); scaling the share by those two ratios (0.28 and 2.1) or by 0.1 moves no moon by more than 3 points (Section 3.3). This top-layer rule holds when D_z is uniform with depth (the base model). When D_z rises with depth fast enough to pass the onset of Eq. 20, the walls rise to the ice instead of sinking, and a deeper layer's hand fills the ocean (Section 2.4); the sampling then counts that layer's molecules (Section 2.6).

The column case begins when D_z < D_h·(H/L)², which is D_z < D_h/333 for Enceladus and D_z < D_h/1386 for Europa (`aniso3d.log`).

**A transition that sweeps as a front.** Everything above assumes that the whole volume passes through the transition together. If the drive instead crosses the water as a slow front, the hand is set in a thin zone at the front and copied as the front advances. In 1D runs (`gradient_ramp.py`) with a front speed v below about one to three times V₀ = √(Dk)(kτ)^(−¼), the volume that counts is a layer of thickness ℓ_A = 2.24(Dvτ/k)^(1/3), independent of the size of the water body (fits within 0.90–1.37 times the measured values); faster fronts act as a uniform transition. This follows the inhomogeneous-quench picture of Kibble and Volovik (1997) and Dziarmaga et al. (1999). The crossover u = v/V₀ between 1 and 3 was found after the runs (the pre-registered value was 3.5). A flat front in 2D and 3D behaves the same way (`front2d.py`, pre-registered): for u ≤ 1 the deciding volume is ℓ_A times the whole width of the front (measured over predicted 0.70–1.30 in 2D and 0.86–1.16 in 3D), and at u = 0.3 it does not grow when the strip is made four times longer, while at u = 3 it does, so in 2D the crossover lies between 0.3 and 3. Wide fronts seldom split into side-by-side regions of opposite hand (5–7% of runs at a width of 8ξ). Halving the grid spacing raised the measured layer by up to 30%, still inside the predicted range. Section 4 tests whether the oceans cross the transition as a front; the ocean results assume a uniform transition.

**Eddy diffusion.** D_h and D_z are eddy, not molecular, diffusivities: above the eddy size, eddies move α as diffusion would. Stirring therefore raises the number of molecules that share one outcome (N in Eq. 12), so faster stirring raises Δ rather than tearing walls apart, and walls move by the eddy diffusivity (Eq. 16). This needs the wall width √(D/k) to exceed the eddy size. I tested this in 2D (`advect2d.py`, pre-registered), replacing the diffusivity by a chaotic stirring flow with the same measured D_eff = 8 and eddies of 0.3, 0.9 and 3.6 wall widths, in a box of 7.2 coherence lengths. The probability that the favoured hand wins followed Φ(aΔ) with a = 1.02 ± 0.12, 0.86 ± 0.11 and 1.10 ± 0.13, against 0.93 ± 0.11 with no flow at the same D_eff (`advect2d.log:26–37`). For eddies of 3.6 wall widths, however, walls survived the transition in 29% of runs (1.6% with diffusion; `advect2d.log:51, 54`) and took longer to clear than Eq. 17 gives. In pre-registered follow-ups (`h14c_advect.py`, `h14d_advect.py`, `h14e_advect.py`, `h14f_advect.py`, building on `h14b_advect.py`) the median clearing time was 3 times Eq. 17's at 3.6 wall widths (`h14c_advect.log:97`), 12 times at 30 wall widths with a molecular share of 1/32 (`h14c_advect.log:75`) and, at 10 wall widths, 6 to 29 times as the molecular share of the diffusivity fell from 1/8 to 1/4096 (`h14e_advect.log:51`, `h14f_advect.log:53-63`); the factor was still growing, and the real ratio of total to molecular diffusivity, about 10⁹, was not reached (Section 4). The diffusion description of the outcome is therefore supported; the diffusion description of wall motion is not, once eddies exceed the wall width, and how far it fails at the real ratio is not shown.

**How often eddies exceed the wall.** The sweep does not draw an eddy size, so I counted the sampled input sets whose wall width √(D_h/k) is smaller than a chosen eddy size (`h14_eddy.py`, pre-registered). Taking the eddy size as the Rhines scale √(2U/β) of a 0.02 m s⁻¹ flow (Ashkenazy and Tziperman, 2021), with β = 2Ω/R (Ω the rotation rate, assumed synchronous, and R the radius), eddies are larger than the wall in 16% of the draws for Mimas, 18% for Enceladus, 29% for Europa, 39% for Callisto and 19% for the early-Earth ocean (`h14_eddy.log:26–40`). Published sizes give 10% for Enceladus (3 km; Kang et al., 2022; Zhang et al., 2024), 23–31% for Europa (20–50 km; Ashkenazy and Tziperman, 2021) and 13–42% for the early-Earth ocean (present-day eddy radii of 25–250 km; Faghmous et al., 2015). The share reaches half only for eddies at the Rhines scale of a 1 m s⁻¹ flow, and then only for Callisto, Ganymede, Oberon, Pluto, Titan and Triton. In 54–69% of the draws with eddies larger than the wall (38% for the early Earth) the eddy is more than 3.6 wall widths, the largest ratio at which the outcome rule was tested; wall clearing was measured up to 30 (above; `h14_eddy.log:60–74`). These shares come from the sweep's priors and from scalings, not from ocean models, except for the three published sizes.

### 2.4 Walls, coarsening and why walls stop

**What a wall is.** After the transition (λ > 0), each region of the ocean sits near α = +1 or α = −1. Where two such regions meet, α passes from one value to the other across a thin layer: a wall, or domain boundary. Its width is set by the balance between the reaction, which pushes α towards ±1, and mixing, which smooths it; it is of order √(D/k). Because Eq. 14 (with g = 0) is a gradient flow, a wall carries an energy per unit area σ, which scales as σ ∝ √D (`two_zone.py` docstring).

**Why walls move.** In this kind of gradient flow, a wall moves along its normal at a speed equal to the diffusivity times its mean curvature (Allen and Cahn, 1979):

$$v_n = D\,\kappa, \tag{16}$$

where κ is the sum of the two principal curvatures. A closed patch therefore shrinks and disappears, and the typical patch size grows as

$$l = A\sqrt{D\,t}. \tag{17}$$

The sampling uses A = 5.5, a rounded fit to 3D runs (`coarsen3d.py: coarsen()`; results in Section 3.2). Because the dynamics do not conserve the amount of either hand, the hand that fills more of the volume expands to fill it, so a patchwork of horizontal size L coarsens to a single domain in a time (L/A)²/D_h. In the 1D runs, the patch size just after the transition follows l ∝ √D·γ^(−¼) (`domains.py: main()`, check A), but in 3D this law fails (31% spread, `domains3d.log`) because curved walls keep moving after the transition; I therefore use Eq. 17 instead.

The bias also moves a flat wall: in 1D the region of the PVED-favoured hand advances at

$$v = \frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\,g\sqrt{D} \tag{18}$$

(`domains.py: wall_speed()`, matched within 10% in two runs). Eq. 18 is used only here, to show that bias-driven wall motion can be neglected: for g ≈ 10⁻¹⁷ this speed is negligible against curvature-driven motion, so I neglect it in the ocean model.

**Why walls stop.** Equation 16 gives zero speed for a flat wall. In a box that is periodic sideways, flat walls therefore do not move, whether they are horizontal (between stacked layers) or vertical, and the layers persist (`aniso3d.py`; results in Section 3.2). On a sphere, a wall at constant depth is itself a sphere of radius r, with κ = 2/r, and its normal is vertical, so only vertical mixing moves it:

$$v = \frac{2D_z}{r}. \tag{19}$$

Each layer wall sinks, the top layer grows, and it expands to fill the full depth in about H·R/(2D_z), with R = L/π (`sphere_layers.py: wall_speed()`; results in Section 3.2).

A wall also stops where the mixing changes with depth. Its energy E = 4πr² σ(r) must fall as it moves. Sinking shrinks its area, which lowers E, but sinking into faster-mixing water raises σ, which raises E. The wall can pass only if

$$\left|\frac{d\ln\sigma}{dr}\right| < \frac{2}{r}. \tag{20}$$

I test Eq. 20 in radial gradient-flow runs (`two_zone.py: run()`) with a slow-mixing layer at the top, middle or bottom, a sharp or gradual joint between the layers, and a bias pushing the wall (`polar_gap.py`); results are in Section 3.4.

**Past the onset, walls reverse.** In a second set of radial runs (`pin_onset.py`), D_z rises smoothly with depth as D(r) = D_top·exp(2G(R − r)), so that |d ln σ/dr| = G everywhere and Eq. 20 predicts the onset at x = Gr/2 = 1. The measured onset is x = 0.9999 ± 0.0001 (n = 8; 0.9998–1.0124 across shell radii of 2–64 depths and two wall widths). Below the onset (x ≤ 0.9) the wall sank to the floor in 42 of 42 runs; past it (x ≥ 1.1) the wall rose to the ice in 54 of 54 runs, and none stalled. A wall past the onset therefore does not stop; it reverses, and the deeper hand fills the ocean. A wall stops only where the mixing jumps over a distance shorter than the wall width (Section 3.4).

With several stacked walls the same condition sets the outcome. With mixing that rises at a constant rate G, the onset sits at the radius r* = 4H/ln ρ, where ρ is the bottom-to-top ratio of D_z: walls above r* rise to the ice, walls below it sink to the floor, and the layer containing r* (the bottom layer if every wall is past the onset) fills the shell; when that layer is thinner than about one wall width, the decision passes to the layer above. I test this in radial runs with up to 10 layers (`multiwall.py`, `deephand_scale.py`, `handoff.py`, `h24_handoff.py`); results are in Section 3.2.

### 2.5 The stratified-lid test

Seawater near its freezing point normally becomes denser as it cools, so cooling at the top drives convection. Below a threshold salinity S*, the thermal expansion coefficient at the freezing point changes sign: cooled water becomes lighter, and the ocean stacks a stratified upper layer on a convecting interior (Zeng and Jansen, 2021, 2024). Such a layer has a much smaller D_z than the water below it, so by Eq. 20 it traps the wall between the top layer and the interior, and the ocean keeps two hands.

I define S* as the salinity at which the thermal expansion coefficient at the freezing temperature is zero,

$$\alpha_T\!\left(S^*,\; T_f(S^*, p),\; p\right) = 0, \qquad p = \rho_{ice}\, g_s\, h_{ice}, \tag{21}$$

computed from the TEOS-10 equation of state (McDougall and Barker, 2011) at the pressure p at the top of the ocean, where h_ice is the ice thickness, g_s the surface gravity (1.315 m/s² for Europa) and ρ_ice = 920 kg/m³ (`rerun_base.py`, `robust_ranking.py`); `verify_sstar.py: sstar()` recomputes S* independently from the same pressure (24.2 MPa for Europa under 20 km of ice). S* is 22.5 g/kg at the top of the Enceladus ocean (18.0 at its floor), and 3.0 g/kg under 20 km of ice on Europa (19.5 under 4 km). The sampling uses S* = 20 g/kg for Enceladus and 3.0 g/kg for Europa (`strat_sweep.py: SALT`).

There is a pressure cutoff. Under more than about 28 MPa of ice, water at its freezing point sinks as it cools at any salinity, so no lid can form (`verify_sstar.py` finds no S* above 28–29 MPa). On Europa this corresponds to an ice shell of about 23 km.

TEOS-10 describes Earth-like seawater, which is mostly sodium chloride. Sulfate- or carbonate-rich oceans, possible on Europa and Ganymede, would move S*. I have no equation of state for them, so I scaled S* by 0.5–4 with the 28 MPa cutoff kept (`lid_melt.py`, predictions written before the run). Europa under 20 km of ice then scores 36% to 18% (30% at the TEOS-10 value), Europa with the Juno shell of Section 3.5 37% to 33%, and Enceladus 13% at half its S*, 5.5% at three quarters and 0% from its S* upward. Ganymede has no lid at any scale, so the order Ganymede, Europa, Enceladus does not change (`lid_melt.log`).

Each input set is given a salinity drawn log-uniformly (Enceladus 5–20 g/kg, the measured range of Postberg et al. (2009); Europa 1–100 g/kg, an assumed prior). A set with S < S* is counted as pinned, and pinned sets are not counted as successes (`strat_sweep.py: main()`). Worlds other than Enceladus and Europa that have no measured salinity get the same log-uniform prior of 1–100 g/kg. I report the lid results beside the lid-free results, not in place of them.

Whether such a lid traps a wall is tested with the radial runs of Section 2.4 and with axisymmetric hemisphere runs in which the lid cuts vertical mixing only, with a polar gap or a single overturning current added in separate runs (Section 3.4). I wrote a prediction before every run and kept the wrong ones.

### 2.6 From the equations to the reported percentages

Every input to Eqs. 11–21 is uncertain, most by orders of magnitude (Table 1). For each world I draw 200,000 input sets, log-uniformly over each range unless noted, with a fixed random seed of 0 (`uncertainty.py: logu()`, `sweep()`).

**Table 1.** Input ranges for the three reference oceans.

| Input | Enceladus | Europa | Early Earth ocean |
|---|---|---|---|
| bias g = PVED/kT | 4.4×10⁻¹⁸ – 2.64×10⁻¹⁶ (0.10–6.2 aeV) sampled; published values reach 4.8×10⁻¹⁶ (11.4 aeV), Section 2.1 | same | same |
| rate-bias factor η (assumed) | 0.1 – 10 | same | same |
| rate k₂ (M⁻¹ s⁻¹) | 10⁻⁶ – 1 | same | same |
| feedstock concentration a (M) | 10⁻¹² – 10⁻⁴ | 10⁻¹² – 10⁻⁴ | 10⁻⁷ – 3×10⁻⁴ |
| product/feedstock ratio p/a (assumed) | 0.01 – 1 | same | same |
| racemization rate k_rac (s⁻¹) | 9.13×10⁻¹⁵ (alanine, 273 K) | same | same |
| horizontal D_h (m² s⁻¹) | 10⁻² – 10² | 10⁻² – 10² | 10² – 10⁴ |
| vertical D_z (m² s⁻¹), depth mean | 10⁻⁹ – 10⁻³ | 10⁻⁹ – 10⁻³ | 10⁻⁶ – 10⁻⁴ |
| bottom/top D_z ratio ρ (assumed) | 1 – 100 | same | same |
| time τ (yr) | 10⁶ – 10⁹ | 10⁸ – 4×10⁹ | 10⁷ – 5×10⁸ |
| volume V (m³) | 2.7×10¹⁶, ×U(0.7, 1.3) | 3.3×10¹⁸, ×U(0.7, 1.3) | 1.332×10¹⁸, ×U(0.7, 1.3) |
| length L = π·R (R in km) | R = 232 | R = 1460 | R = 6371 |
| chemistry factor F | U(0.3, 0.7) | same | same |
| salinity (g/kg), S\* | 5–20, S\* = 20 | 1–100, S\* = 3.0 | no lid |

The vertical range is the one modelled for icy-moon oceans by Zeng and Jansen (2021), with its lower end raised from 10⁻¹⁰ to 10⁻⁹ m² s⁻¹, about the molecular diffusivity of small solutes. An energy budget gives a physical check on this range (`tidal_mix.py`, pre-registered), following the argument of Munk and Wunsch (1998). Mixing a stratified ocean lifts dense water, and the work comes from tidal power. For a linear density profile the stored potential energy per unit area is ρN²H³/12, with N² = Δb/H and Δb the top-to-bottom buoyancy difference. If a fraction Γ of the ocean tidal power P goes into mixing, K_z = ΓP/(ρAΔb), with A the ocean area (Munk and Wunsch's Eq. 3.15 solved for the diffusivity), and the mixing time is H²/(12K_z). I take P from Chen et al. (2014) and Hay and Matsuyama (2019), Γ = 0.2 (0.1–0.3), the upper bound of Osborn (1980) adopted by Munk and Wunsch (1998) and the value used for icy moons by Zeng and Jansen (2021) and Jansen et al. (2023) (Munk and Wunsch note that lower values have been measured in salt-stratified water, which would lower K_z in proportion; Section 3.3 tests Γ = 0.05), and for every world the interior density contrast Δρ/ρ = 2×10⁻⁶ modelled for Europa (Ashkenazy and Tziperman, 2021). The mid estimates of K_z are above 10⁻³ m² s⁻¹ for Europa, Ganymede, Titan, Callisto, Triton and Mimas, and between 1.7×10⁻⁸ (Enceladus) and 4.7×10⁻⁴ m² s⁻¹ (Miranda) for the other seven moons with a published tidal power (`tidal_mix.log`, Section 2). The same budget reproduces Zeng and Jansen's (2021) range for the Enceladus lid (3.4×10⁻¹⁰ to 3.4×10⁻³ m² s⁻¹ against their 3×10⁻¹⁰ to 3×10⁻³). The main results keep the range of Table 1; the energy-budget range is a sensitivity test (Section 3.3). The Enceladus volume is derived from ice-shell and core geometry (Čadek et al., 2016). The feedstock concentration a sets the copying rate k = k₂a, and the product concentration p sets N (Eq. 12). An input set is counted as a success, meaning that the PVED-favoured hand is selected across the whole ocean with probability of at least 97.7%, only if all the conditions below hold:

1. the reaction finishes and outpaces racemization: k₂aτ ≥ 10 and k₂a > 2k_rac. The second part is only a necessary condition for the network of Eq. 2. With all rate constants equal and the feed a₀ ending at 1.6 times the value at which the growth rate of u passes through zero (Section 2.2.2), a chiral steady state exists only if k₂a is about 5.7 times 2k_rac, and it reaches a purity of 0.9 only above about 22 times, because outflow and feed depletion also count against the amplifier (`kcheck.py`; `kcheck.log:11–12`). The 5.7 moves from 3.1 to 26 over the cross-inhibition and uncatalysed rates, which Table 1 does not vary (`kcheck.log:15–17`). The main runs keep the weaker test; Section 4 gives the effect of the stricter one;
2. either (i) H' ≤ L, the patchwork coarsens horizontally in time, (L/5.5)²/D_h ≤ τ, and F·Δ(f = 1) ≥ 2;
   or (ii) H' > L, (L/5.5)²/D_h ≤ τ, and the layers merge in time. If the top wall is below the Eq. 20 onset, the layers merge by sinking, H·(L/π)/(2D_z) ≤ τ, and F·Δ(f = min(1, L/H')) ≥ 2 for the top layer. If it is past the onset, the walls above r* rise and those below sink (Section 2.4); the rise and the sinking must finish within τ, and F·Δ ≥ 2 for the molecules of the layer containing r*;
3. for the lid test only: S ≥ S*.

In case (i), vertical coarsening, (H/5.5)²/D_z ≤ τ, follows from H' ≤ L and does not need a separate test.

**Additions to the sampling.** The base model (`uncertainty.py: sweep()`) used g = 4×10⁻¹⁹ to 4×10⁻¹⁶, D_z uniform with depth from 10⁻¹⁰ m² s⁻¹, one concentration for both k and N, and no racemization. I call its results the *uniform-D_z base model*. The present sampling (`sweep2.py`, then `sweep3.py` and `multiwall.py`) reuses its 200,000 draws per world with seed 0 (common random numbers) and adds, in order: the published PVED range; the rate-bias factor η; the D_z floor; racemization (Bada and Schroeder (1975) for free alanine at 3 °C, as tabulated by Steen et al. (2013), taken to 273 K with their activation energy of 93 kJ mol⁻¹; aspartic acid, 2.73×10⁻¹⁴ s⁻¹, as a sensitivity; every world is taken at 273 K, an assumption); the ratio p/a; and D_z rising with depth as a smooth exponential with bottom-to-top ratio ρ. This profile is an assumption. If mixing is instead strongest near the top, walls sink as in the base model and these results do not apply. The drawn D_z is taken as the profile's depth mean (assumed), and the times for walls to rise or sink use the closed form for constant D_z and constant G. Draws in which a second wall is also past the onset are counted as no single hand in `sweep3.py`; `multiwall.py` gives them the hand of the layer containing r* (the bottom layer if every wall is past the onset), as measured in Section 3.2. In the test runs the simulated time was up to 5.1 times this closed form (median 1.2), so I also report the shares with these times multiplied by 10, the sensitivity written before the runs, and quote that value as the lower end of each range. The reported percentage, which I call the success share, is the share of input sets that meet all the conditions. It is not the probability that the bias set life's hand. It is the share of plausible conditions under which the bias, rather than chance, controls the outcome.

To show which input matters most, I split the draws at the median of each input and recompute the share in each half (`uncertainty.py: main()`). Results are in Sections 3.3–3.5.

Twelve more worlds use Europa's ranges for a, p/a, D_h, D_z, ρ, g, η, k₂ and F, an assumption since no measurement tells the worlds apart on chemistry or mixing; only body size, ocean depth, ice thickness, age and salinity change. Ocean depths come from published interior models: Ganymede (Saur et al., 2015), Callisto (Vance et al., 2018), Titan (Sohl et al., 2014), Triton (Hansen et al., 2021), Pluto (Johnson et al., 2016), the Uranian moons (Castillo-Rogez et al., 2023), Dione (Beuthe et al., 2016), Mimas (Lainey et al., 2024) and Miranda (Strom et al., 2024). Each world except Enceladus and Europa uses V = 4πR²H (times the same U(0.7, 1.3) factor) and L = πR, with age log-uniform. I also rerun each world at the shallow and deep ends of its published depth range.

### 2.7 Why the results should be believed, and what is assumed

Table 2 lists the checks of each step; `run_all.sh` in the code repository reruns them, apart from the long runs named under Code availability. VERIFICATION.md records the checks of an earlier version of the model (2026-10-06).

**Table 2.** Checks that support each step.

| Step | Check | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Eq. 10 (normal form, linear analysis) | 4,000 brute-force stochastic runs of Eq. 6 per point (`sim.py: simulate()`) | agree within ±0.005 (n = 4,000 per point, SE ≤ 0.008); the script's assertion tolerance is 0.03 |
| Eqs. 3–5 (reduction from chemistry) | full Frank network with chemical Langevin noise (`frank.py`), 2,000 runs per point | predicted/simulated P (simulated ± SE, n = 2,000): 0.500/0.485 ± 0.011, 0.737/0.733 ± 0.010, 0.858/0.854 ± 0.008, 0.999/0.974 ± 0.004, 0.909/0.898 ± 0.007 for slow sweeps (τ ≥ 800); a fast sweep (τ = 200) gave 0.628/0.680 ± 0.010, so the formula is conservative there |
| Same, second chemistry | reversible network (`frank2.py`), 2,000 runs per point | 0.500/0.493 ± 0.011, 0.802/0.785 ± 0.009, 0.967/0.952 ± 0.005, 0.951/0.948 ± 0.005; F = 0.71, within 1.7 points |
| Selected state stays selected | reaction held at saturation for 100 times longer than the selection window | share on the favoured hand stayed at 0.730 ± 0.007 (n = 4,000); the flip rate matches Kramers' escape rate (√2/2π)·e^(−1/(2ε)) within 5% (Kramers, 1940); Section 3.8 |
| Eq. 17 (coarsening) | 3D runs (`coarsen3d.py`) | exponents 0.45, 0.49 (Section 3.2); halving the grid spacing changed patch sizes by < 5% |
| Eq. 19 (layer walls on a sphere) | `sphere_layers.py` | within 1.6% of 2D_z/r; independent of D_h (Section 3.2) |
| Eq. 20 (pinning) | `two_zone.py`, predictions written before the runs | no-layer control at 1.043 times the predicted time (1.023 at half the time step); with a sharp joint (width 0.02, narrower than the wall, about 0.07), pinning between ratios 1.02 and 1.05 |
| Eq. 20 onset, smooth profile | `pin_onset.py`, 8 configurations; 96-run fate scan | onset at 0.9999 ± 0.0001 of Eq. 20; walls past it reverse (54/54), walls below it sink (42/42), 0 stalled; halving dz and dt shifts it by < 0.0001 |
| Which layer's hand fills (Sections 2.4, 3.2) | `multiwall.py`, 336 + 108 radial runs | deepest layer 336/336; layer containing r* 94/108 (87.0 ± 3.2%); 63/63 when that layer is ≥ 5 wall widths thick (post hoc); with walls down to 1/400 of the depth, 365/365 at ≥ 3 wall widths (`deephand_scale.py`, 600 runs); next-layer-up handoff below one wall width predicts the hand in 93.3 ± 1.0% (`handoff.py`); a rule frozen before testing, 96.5 ± 0.8% on 570 new runs (`h24_handoff.py`) |
| Patch sum (Sections 2.3, 3.2) | `kz_patches.py`, `kz_large.py`, runs of Eq. 14 through the transition | c = 0.92 ± 0.02 (1D, about 80 patches; 0.88 ± 0.02 at 10–40 patches; `kz_large.py`), 0.98 ± 0.05 (2D); theory 0.91, 0.96 |
| Front (Section 2.3) | `gradient_ramp.py`, 1D; `front2d.py`, 2D and 3D | ℓ_A = 2.24(Dvτ/k)^(1/3) within 0.90–1.37 of the measured layer for slow fronts (1D); ℓ_A × front width within 0.70–1.30 (2D) and 0.86–1.16 (3D) for u ≤ 1, independent of system length |
| Eq. 21 (S*) | independent TEOS-10 recomputation (`verify_sstar.py`, gsw 3.6.23) | matches to the rounding of the ice pressure (Europa 3.2 vs 3.0 g/kg at 24.2 MPa; Dione 5.75 vs 5.6) |
| Sampling (Section 2.6) | seeds 1–5 (`verify_seeds.py`); two blind rebuilds of an earlier version of the model from its text alone (2026-10-06, VERIFICATION.md) | every world within 0.3 points of seed 0; the first rebuild missed by up to 7.5 points because the text left out inputs, since added; the second came within 0.4 points of that version |
| Energy-budget mixing (Section 2.6) | `tidal_mix.py` | K_z × mixing time = H²/12 to 1.000000 for every world; the full-range rerun reproduces `multiwall.log` and `seedfix.log` exactly; Zeng and Jansen's (2021) lid range reproduced |
| Wider bias top (Section 2.1) | `g_wide.py` | the rerun at the old top (2.64×10⁻¹⁶) reproduces 150 of 150 stored values of `multiwall.log` and `seedfix.log` (`g_wide.log:16`) |
| Racemization test against the network (Sections 2.6, 4) | `kcheck.py` | the antisymmetric eigenvalue equals a_rac − 1 − 2r to 5×10⁻¹¹ (`kcheck.log:9`); with no extra mask the rerun reproduces the stored multiwall and sweep3 values for all 15 worlds (`kcheck.log:40`) |
| Persistence of a pure pool (Section 4) | `persist.py` | the pure-state purity from the ODE equals the closed formula to 6 digits (`persist.log:24–37`); the D-pure start in a D-preferring world gives the exact mirror (`persist.log:64, 114`) |
| Explicit eddies against eddy diffusion (Sections 2.3, 4) | `advect2d.py` | the eddy diffusivity measured from tracers is 6.95–7.10 against a design 7.0 for all three eddy sizes, and 8.05–8.35 against 8.0 from the decay of a long wave (`advect2d.log:20–23`); a well-mixed check (3 cells, D = 4) gives P = 0.848 ± 0.006 against Φ(Δ) = 0.841 (`advect2d.log:2`) |
| Energy-budget mixing at Γ = 0.05 (Section 3.3) | `tidal_mix_g005.py` | the full-range rerun reproduces `multiwall.log` and `seedfix.log` exactly; 14 of 15 band rows of `tidal_mix.log` reproduced digit for digit (the 15th, early Earth, is skipped by a parsing error in the check; its shares are identical) |
| Counting rule at lab scale | reanalysis of published amplifiers (Section 3.7) | unbiased runs split as chance predicts; stirred NaClO₃ acts as n_eff ≈ 1.0–1.1 pools |

The numerical scheme for all spatial runs is explicit Euler–Maruyama time stepping with second-order centred differences (the 7-point Laplacian). Boundaries are periodic sideways, and the top and bottom are closed. Units are 1/k for time and √(D/k) for length, with grid spacing 1 and a time step of 0.05 (dt = min(0.05, 0.1/D) in `domains3d.py: sweep3d()` and `coarsen3d.py: coarsen()`), which meets the 3D stability limit D·dt/dx² ≤ 1/6. The radial runs of `pin_onset.py` and `multiwall.py` instead use a finite-volume operator with implicit diffusion (dz = 0.002, dt = 0.1). Thirteen predictions written before the runs in `breakeven.py`, `assumed_rules.py` and `earth_breakeven.py` all held (VERIFICATION.md in the code repository). Of the 326 predictions written before the newer runs, 249 held (76%); the tally for each script, with notes on borderline cases, is listed under Code availability, and each script's docstring lists every failed prediction. Later pre-registered runs are reported in the text against their stated thresholds.

**Main assumptions.**

1. *An amplifying chemistry exists.* No measured prebiotic reaction is known to amplify handedness in this way. The model asks what such a reaction would need, not whether it exists.
2. *The bias is the PVED, as a rate bias.* Eq. 1 identifies the fractional rate bias with ΔE_pv/kT. How the energy difference of the molecules enters the rate constants is not derived here; a factor of order 1 in this identification is small against the range sampled (g over 1.8 orders of magnitude, times η over 2). Strictly, the rate bias is the transition-state minus the reactant PVED, averaged over conformers, so the identification holds only to order of magnitude, and a sign flip or near-cancellation lies outside the sampled range (Section 2.1).
3. *Mixing is diffusive.* Eddies are represented by D_h and D_z. Currents that carry a wall are tested separately (Section 3.4).
4. *The top layer has thickness about L in stretched units* (Section 2.3, case ii). Section 3.3 tests the two assumed rules (`assumed_rules.py`).
5. *Uniform priors in log space.* The percentages depend on the input ranges of Table 1.
6. *Salinity and lid are independent of the other inputs,* and a pinned set never counts as a success.
7. *Vertical mixing rises with depth* (Section 2.6), as a smooth exponential with bottom-to-top ratio ρ = 1–100. This is measured for Earth's ocean below the surface mixed layer (Ledwell et al., 2000; Waterhouse et al., 2014). No measurement constrains the direction in any ice-covered ocean: models give a convective interior with nearly uniform mixing (Zeng and Jansen, 2021; Bire et al., 2022), a stratified layer under the ice (the lid case, Section 2.5), or dissipation in boundary layers at the ice, the seafloor or both (Jansen et al., 2023; Soderlund et al., 2024). Where mixing falls with depth (ρ < 1), no wall pins and the result equals the uniform-D_z sampling with all other additions on. Section 3.3 gives the moons with ρ drawn from 0.01 to 100.
8. *The transition happens everywhere at once,* not as a slow front (Section 2.3); if the drive comes from vents and spreads by mixing, the 2–17% of draws that cross as a front move no share by more than 0.01 points (Section 4), and the patchwork counts as one pool with f = 1 rather than f = c².

### 2.8 Use of AI tools

An AI assistant (Claude, Anthropic; Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5, Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1, through Claude Code; used 23 September to 21 October 2026 [CONFIRM: add any other use, such as claude.ai chats, and the final end date]; available at https://claude.ai) was used to write the simulation, analysis and plotting code. I directed the work, chose the questions, models and inputs, and checked all results and numbers myself. The scripts check their results with assertions or against predictions written before each run (Section 2.7); a few only print their results for comparison with the text (Code availability). All figures are drawn by these scripts. The use of the assistant for writing the text is declared in the Acknowledgements. The AI assistant is not an author.

## 3 Results

### 3.1 How many molecules must react together?

Setting Δ = 2 in Eq. 11 gives the number of molecules that must react as one well-mixed pool, Eq. 13. For g = 10⁻¹⁷ (near the low end of the published amino-acid range) and a reaction that only just finishes (kτ = 10), N ≥ 3.6×10³³ molecules, or 6×10⁹ mol. At 1 µM that is a well-mixed cube of water 18 km on a side; at 1 mM, 1.8 km; at 0.1 M, 0.4 km. A 1 m³ pond at 1 M holds only 6×10²⁶ molecules, about 7 orders of magnitude too few.

Over the range sampled (Section 2.1), N runs from 1.8×10³⁴ at the smallest PVED (g = 4.4×10⁻¹⁸) to 5.1×10³⁰ at the largest (g = 2.64×10⁻¹⁶, alanine). In plain terms: a pond is too small at every value sampled, short by a factor of 3×10⁷ to 9×10³ (1 m³ at 1 M). A well-mixed lake of 1 km³ at 1 mM is short by a factor of 31 at the smallest value but passes, with about 100 times more molecules than needed, at the largest; a 10 km³ lake at 1 mM is short by a factor of 3 at the smallest. An ocean holds enough at every value sampled. At the largest value the cube is 2.0 km on a side at 1 µM, 200 m at 1 mM and 44 m at 0.1 M; at the smallest, 31 km, 3.1 km and 670 m (`sweep2.log`).

The PVED is a fixed energy, so g = ΔE/kT is larger in the cold. By Eq. 13 the needed N scales as T²: 3.6×10³³ at 0 °C, 4.3×10³³ at 27 °C and 6.7×10³³ at 100 °C. Cooling from 100 °C to 0 °C lowers N by a factor of about 2. A longer reaction time lowers N only as (kτ)^(−½): a sweep 10,000× slower lowers N by 100×. Concentration alone cannot make a small setting large enough. Only ocean-scale water, or a large lake at the top of the published PVED range, that is mixed within the selection window can pool enough molecules (Figure 3).

The same rule applies to connected compartments, such as the pores of a hydrothermal mound. I simulated 16 compartments, each with 1/16 of the molecules, exchanging material at rate q (normal form, Eq. 6, with g = 10⁻³, γ = 0.01). The probability that the majority ended on the favoured hand (n = 3,000 runs per point, ± one standard error √(p(1−p)/n)) was 0.675 ± 0.009 when they were isolated, 0.686 ± 0.008 when q times the length of the selection window was 0.1, 0.724 ± 0.008 at 1 and 0.714 ± 0.008 at 10 (`review.log`, R3). From Eq. 10 directly, a single pool holding all the molecules gives 0.724, and one isolated compartment gives 0.559. In a vent mound (k₂ = 10⁻³ M⁻¹ s⁻¹, c = 1 mM, 10⁵ yr), the selection window lasts about 56 years. In that time diffusion links only about a 2 m³ pond, which gives P = 0.500, a coin flip. If fluid flow connects a 100 m mound (10⁶ m³), P = 0.731. A flow-connected cubic kilometre gives P = 1.000 (these values are from Eq. 10, not simulated). Compartments raise P only in proportion to the volume that flow links within the selection window.

![Figure 3 Minimum reactant concentration for the PVED-favoured hand to be selected, against the bias g, at k₂ = 10⁻³ M⁻¹ s⁻¹.](../threshold2.png)

**Figure 3.** Minimum reactant concentration for the PVED-favoured hand to be selected, against the bias g = ΔE/kT, at k₂ = 10⁻³ M⁻¹ s⁻¹. The lines are for a small lake (1 km² × 10 m) and for the Enceladus, early-Earth and Europa oceans, each treated as fully mixed. The grey band is the amino-acid range sampled in Table 1 (4.4×10⁻¹⁸ to 2.64×10⁻¹⁶, 0.10–6.2 aeV). Sloped parts are set by the size condition; flat parts are set by the time condition (k₂cτ ≥ 10). Mixing and layering (Sections 3.2–3.4) are not included.

### 3.2 Selection proceeds in patches, then in layers

Real oceans mix slowly, so selection happens in many patches at once. In 3D, curved walls straighten, small patches shrink, and patch size grows as l = A√(Dt) (Eq. 17), with exponents of 0.45 and 0.49 (theory 0.5) and A = 5.48 and 5.60 at two mixing strengths (`coarsen3d.log`). From a 55% majority, the majority hand filled 93.0% of a 128³ cube by t = 400 (`coarsen3d.log`) and 99% of a 96³ cube (Figure 4, top).

**Patches add up to nearly one pool.** In runs of Eq. 14 through the transition (`kz_patches.py`), the patchwork behaves as one pool with Δ reduced by a factor c: c = 0.92 ± 0.02 in 1D for a single system of about 80 patches (theory 0.91; `kz_large.py`, pre-registered), 5.7 standard errors above the value 0.80 for independent box patches (smaller systems of 10–40 patches gave 0.88 ± 0.02 pooled, ± 0.03 each; the pre-registered pool of all three sizes gives 0.90 ± 0.015, 6.8 standard errors above 0.80), and c = 0.98 ± 0.05 in 2D (theory 0.96). In 3D the theory gives c = 0.98; this is not simulated. The majority hand at the end of the sweep was kept through later coarsening in at least 99% of 1D runs, and in 2D coarsening left P unchanged (0.844 against 0.843). Patch formation of this kind is the Kibble–Zurek picture (Zurek, 1985). Since the sampling uses f = 1 rather than f = c² (0.84 in 1D, about 0.96 in 2D and 3D), it overstates Δ by about 2% in 3D.

Weak vertical mixing changes this. After stretching (Eq. 15), the ocean is deeper than it is wide for 92–96% of the plausible range. I ran `aniso3d.py` (L = 32, periodic sideways, closed top and bottom) with four runs from each of two starts, 50% and 55% of the volume in the favoured hand (`aniso3d.log`):

- tall column (H' = 128): the patchwork froze into 3–5 stacked layers in 7 of 8 runs (4 of 4 from 50%, 3 of 4 from 55%), unchanged from t = 500 to 1000;
- cube (H' = 32): one hand in 4 of 4 runs from 55%; from 50%, one hand in 3 of 4 (in one of them the unfavoured hand) and stacked layers in 1;
- shallow slab (H' = 8 < L): one hand in 2 of 4 runs from each start; the other 4 runs stopped at favoured shares of 0.53, 0.50, 0.59 and 0.56, divided by two flat vertical walls spanning the box, unchanged from t = 200 to 1000.

Figure 4 (bottom) shows layers that do not change from t = 400 to t = 5000. The slab runs test case (i) of Section 2.3 (a shallow ocean coarsens to a single domain, f = 1). A flat wall spanning a sideways-periodic box has zero curvature and does not move (Eq. 16), so half the slab runs kept two hands. A sphere has no stable wall: a great circle has zero mean curvature but is unstable, and on a sphere all 664 random starts reached one hand, the last one after 36 times the sweep's sideways time (`h13_sphere_late136.log`).

On a sphere, the layers must merge by sinking. Measured sink speeds were 0.991, 0.991 and 0.984 times 2·D_z/r (Eq. 19), with 0.0% change when D_h was quadrupled (`sphere_layers.log`). At the sampling floor of D_z = 10⁻⁹ m² s⁻¹, the top layer would take up to 1.5×10¹¹ yr (Enceladus) or 2.8×10¹² yr (Europa) to fill the depth by sinking (ten times longer at 10⁻¹⁰), far longer than the age of either ocean, and the merge-in-time condition holds in 39% of draws for both moons (`rerun_base.log`). Vertical mixing is therefore the key unknown for icy moons. If D_z rises with depth past the Eq. 20 onset, the layers merge instead by walls rising to the ice (Section 2.4), which is the case for most moon draws (Section 3.3).

**Which layer's hand fills the ocean.** With several stacked walls (`multiwall.py`), the walls never crossed. In 336 runs with 2–5 walls all past the onset, the deepest layer's hand filled the shell in every run, against 52.1% for the largest layer's hand and 41.7% for the top layer's. With mixing that rises at a constant rate G, walls above r* (Section 2.4) rose to the ice, walls below it sank to the floor, and the layer containing r* filled the shell in 94 of 108 runs (87.0 ± 3.2%). In a check made after these runs, it did so in all 63 runs in which that layer was at least five wall widths thick. These were small radial test domains (shell radius 2 or 16 depths, walls 0.035–0.07 depths wide). In 600 further runs with walls down to 1/400 of the depth (`deephand_scale.py`, pre-registered), the layer containing r* filled the shell in all 365 runs in which it was at least three wall widths thick, at every wall width; thinner layers usually lost to the layer above (8.0 ± 2.9% filled below one wall width, 60.8 ± 5.5% at one to two).

**A handoff rule.** Handing the decision to the next layer up whenever the deciding layer is thinner than one wall width predicts the filling hand in 93.3 ± 1.0% of these runs, against 81.0 ± 1.6% for the plain rule; a three-width cut does worse (76.8 ± 1.7%; `handoff.py`). Neither reaches the pre-registered 95%. A rule that also uses the drift of the deciding layer's rising wall and the thickness of the layer above (four coefficients fitted on these 600 runs and frozen before testing; `h24_handoff.py`, pre-registered) predicted the filling hand in 96.5 ± 0.8% of 570 new runs. Where it handed the decision up (104 runs) it was right in 86.5 ± 3.4%. Both meet the pre-registered 95% and 85% (`h24_handoff_test.log:32`). The best cut on thickness alone, tuned on the new runs, reaches 94.7% (`h24_handoff_test.log:61`). The rule was tested with walls 1/200 to 1/29 of the depth and up to 10 layers in the radial model; when every layer is thinner than one wall width, no fixed rule exceeds 56% (68 runs, all artificial thin stacks; `h24_handoff_test.log:64`). The constant-D_z closed form for the rise and sink times is about 2.2 times too fast at the thinnest walls; a correction fitted to these runs changes no share by more than 1.4 points (`deephand_scale.log`).

![Figure 4 Coarsening of regions of opposite hand.](../coarsening.png)

**Figure 4.** Coarsening of regions of opposite hand (blue, favoured hand; red, the other). Top: horizontal slices of a 96³ cube with equal mixing, starting from a 55% majority. Patches round off and shrink, and the favoured hand reaches 99% by t = 400. Bottom: side view of a tall box, which is equivalent to weak vertical mixing. The patchwork freezes into flat layers of opposite hand, unchanged to t = 5000. Right: patch size in a 128³ cube grows as √(Dt). The dashed line is the rounded fit A = 5.5 (Section 3.2). Time is in reaction units.

### 3.3 Success shares for Enceladus, Europa and early Earth

Varying every input of Table 1 at once (Section 2.6), before the lid test, the PVED-favoured hand is selected in at least 67% of plausible cases for early Earth's open ocean, counting draws with a second wall past the onset as no single hand (`sweep3.log`). Giving those draws the hand of the layer containing r* raises the share to 99.9% (`multiwall.log`); at ocean scale this rule holds whenever that layer is at least three wall widths thick, but about half of the deciding layers are thinner (Section 3.2). Weighting each such draw by the measured chance that a layer of its thickness fills the shell, and counting the rest as no hand, gives a floor of 89.0%; handing the decision to the layer above when the deciding layer is thinner than one wall width gives 97.6% (`deephand_scale.log`, `handoff.log`). With the handoff rule of `h24_handoff.py`, which also uses the drift of the rising wall and the thickness of the layer above, early Earth is 98.2% if draws in which no layer is thick enough count as no hand and 99.9% if the deepest layer decides them (`h24_handoff_worlds.log:12`). For the moons, with the rise and sink times multiplied by 10 at the low end and as computed at the high end, the shares are 19–26% for Enceladus and 29–40% for Europa (`multiwall.log`; Enceladus from `seedfix.log`, which corrects a reused random seed). The same handoff puts Enceladus at 23.7% and Europa at 34.9% (26.5% with the lid), inside these ranges (`handoff.log`). In the uniform-D_z base model they were 22%, 29% and 75%, and if layers never merged (the flat-box bound), the moons would fall to 3% and 7% and early Earth to 40% (`uncertainty.log`).

Each addition to the sampling moves the shares as follows (Enceladus / Europa / early Earth, no lid; `sweep2.log`, `sweep3.log`, `multiwall.log`; Enceladus from `seedfix.log`):

- base model: 21.8 / 28.6 / 74.7%;
- published PVED range: 24.1 / 30.4 / 74.7%; with η: 23.8 / 30.0 / 74.7%;
- D_z floor of 10⁻⁹ m² s⁻¹: 27.8 / 35.0 / 74.7% (draws below the floor never succeeded);
- racemization: 27.1 / 31.3 / 74.7% (using the faster racemization of aspartic acid instead of alanine lowers any world by at most 0.7 points at the depth step);
- separate feedstock and product: 24.7 / 30.4 / 74.7%;
- D_z rising with depth, with every draw past the Eq. 20 onset counted as no single hand: 6.7 / 9.3 / 39.9%;
- reversed walls counted, with a second wall past the onset still counted as no single hand: 9.0 / 13.9 / 67.0%;
- the layer containing r* fills the ocean: 26.4 / 39.6 / 99.9% (19.4 / 29.0 / 99.7% with times ×10); early Earth keeps 99.9% if sideways coarsening is slowed by a factor up to about 2600 (99.8 at 3000, 94 at 10,000, 61 at 100,000; Section 4).

Depth-dependent mixing is the largest change. It puts the top wall past the onset in 55–77% of icy-moon draws and 60% of Earth draws (`sweep3.log`), and whether those draws succeed depends on the deepest-hand rule.

**Chance, the PVED, or no single hand.** Each draw ends in one of three ways: one hand set by the PVED (F·Δ ≥ 2), one hand set by chance (F·Δ < 2), or no single hand (the reaction does not finish, racemization is faster, the layers do not merge in time, or a lid pins the wall). Chance sets the hand in at most 14.9% of draws in any world (Umbriel; Enceladus 9.8%, Europa 4.9%, early Earth 0.0%; `multiwall.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`). Among draws that end with one hand, the PVED sets that hand in 73% (Enceladus) to 100% (early Earth) of them, and in 89% for Europa; with the chance-set hands that match it by luck, the PVED-favoured hand is the one in 95% of Europa's (Section 4). Where the bias fails, it is mostly because the ocean ends with no single hand, not because chance sets it.

Splitting the draws of one input at its median, with every other input drawn over its full range (no lid; `rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`), vertical mixing has the largest effect (Figure 5): the share is 4%, 7% and 99.9% (Enceladus, Europa, Earth) in the low half of its range and 48%, 72% and 100% in the high half. Next come concentration (+30 and +20 points for the moons), time (+17 and +12), the rate k₂ (+6 and +9) and the bias g (+6 and +5). Early Earth moves by less than 1 point for every input. Faster sideways mixing lowers the share (−1 to −4 points) only through the assumed top-layer rule f = L/H' (Section 2.3): since H' ∝ √D_h (Eq. 15), a larger D_h shrinks the top layer's share of molecules. This is not a physical result. F and volume each move the moons by 3.3 points or less.

![Figure 5 Effect of each input on the success share.](fig_sensitivity3.png)

**Figure 5.** Effect of each input on the success share for Enceladus, Europa and early Earth, with rise and sink times as computed, before the lid test. Each bar runs from the share among draws with that input below its median (the lower half of its range) to the share among draws above the median, with all other inputs drawn over their full ranges; blue bars rise with the input and red bars fall. The dashed line is the share with every input drawn over its full range. Values are parsed from `rerun_base.log`, with the Enceladus rows corrected from `seedfix.log` (no new runs), by `journals/make_fig_sensitivity3.py`, which asserts that each parsed value reproduces its log line.

**What would the unknowns have to be?** With every other input still drawn over its range, a moon passes 50% almost only through vertical mixing: D_z ≥ 2.6×10⁻⁵ m² s⁻¹ for Enceladus and ≥ 1.7×10⁻⁶ m² s⁻¹ for Europa (8.9×10⁻⁵ and 1.0×10⁻⁵ with times ×10). No tenth of any other input's range takes Enceladus above 44%; for Europa only the longest times (≥ 3.3×10⁹ yr) reach 50%. Even in the fastest tenth of vertical mixing the moons level off at 63% and 80%. Early Earth stays at 99% or above in every tenth of every input (97% with times ×10), so no input has a crossing; near the measured thermocline value of 1.1×10⁻⁵ m² s⁻¹ (Ledwell et al., 1993) its share is 100.0% (`rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`).

**Assumed rules and the bias.** Two rules were assumed rather than measured: the share of molecules in the top layer that sets the outcome (f = L/H', Section 2.3), and the bar Δ ≥ 2. Changing them (scaling the share by 0.1, 0.28 or 2.1, or moving the bar to Δ ≥ 1 or Δ ≥ 3) changes a moon by at most 3 points; counting the whole ocean (f = 1) raises Enceladus by 4 points and Europa by 3. None changes the order. The sampled bias spans 0.10–6.2 aeV (Section 2.1). Raising its top to 11.4 aeV, the largest value of Zanasi et al. (1999), raises each world's share by 0.0 to 1.2 points (Ganymede 33.5–44.1% to 33.7–44.5%, Europa with its lid 22.0–30.1% to 22.2–30.4%, Uranian moons up to 43.6–53.7% to 44.5–54.8%), leaves early Earth at 99.9% and Enceladus with a lid at 0%, and changes no order (`g_wide.log:120–153`). A bias 10× or 100× larger raises Enceladus to 31.6% and 34.8% and Europa to 43.2% and 44.3%. Even an unlimited bias stops at 36.1% and 44.5%, because the reaction must still finish and the layers must still merge in time. Early Earth is 99.8–99.9% in every case (`rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`).

**Slowly swinging conditions.** Tides, orbital cycles and climate make the drive swing while it ramps. A swing only weakens the effective bias. Mild, plausible and harsh swings give 25.7%/38.3%/99.9%, 23.6%/35.8%/99.8% and 19.9%/31.7%/99.4% (Enceladus/Europa/Earth; `rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`). Swinging costs the moons at most 8 points (harsh swings: Enceladus 6.5, Europa 7.9) and early Earth at most 0.5.

**Mixing that weakens with depth.** The moons were rerun with mixing as likely to weaken as to strengthen with depth (ρ log-uniform on 0.01–100; `rho2side.py`, seeds 0–3, pre-registered; Enceladus from `seedfix.log`). Where ρ < 1, the D_z gradient and the wall curvature both push walls downward, so no wall pins and the top layer's hand fills the ocean; these draws equal the uniform-D_z sampling exactly, and each share is the average of that and the rising-only value to within 0.1 point. With times ×10 to times as computed, the shares become Ganymede 31.7–37.1% (rising only: 33.5–44.1%), Titan 25.5–30.7% (25.5–35.8%), Europa 29.8–35.0% without a lid and 22.6–26.6% with one (29.0–39.6% and 22.0–30.1%), and Enceladus 22.1–25.6% without a lid (19.4–26.4%) and 0% with one. The upper end of each moon's range falls by up to 7 points; the lower end moves by less than 3 points for these four moons. The order of the worlds does not change, and chance sets the hand in at most 10% of draws (Umbriel). These sinking times use the uniform-D_z rule; the true, faster sinking where ρ < 1 would raise the moons by up to about 5 points. Early Earth would fall from 99.9% to 87.3%, but its rise with depth is measured, so it keeps ρ = 1–100.

**Mixing set by the tidal energy budget.** With D_z drawn log-uniformly over each moon's energy-budget range (Section 2.6) instead of Table 1's, the share without a lid rises for 12 of the 13 moons with a published tidal power (`tidal_mix.py`, seeds 0–3, pre-registered; times as computed; Enceladus with the seed fix of `seedfix.py`): to 73–81% for Europa, Ganymede, Titan, Callisto, Triton, Titania and Oberon, 63–69% for Umbriel, Miranda and Mimas, 52% for Dione and 41% for Enceladus (from 26.4%). Ariel falls from 49.6% to 44.3%: its range, 6×10⁻⁸ to 2×10⁻⁷ m² s⁻¹, is slow enough that its layers often do not merge in time (about 2×10⁹ yr at the mid value; `tidal_mix.log:33`). With the lid test, Europa rises from 30.1% to 60.4%, Dione, Miranda and Mimas end at 20–32%, and Enceladus stays at 0%. Pluto, with no published ocean tidal power, and early Earth keep their own ranges. Seeds 1–3 change any share by at most 0.37 points. For six moons and part of the Enceladus and Miranda ranges the energy-budget values lie above 10⁻³ m² s⁻¹, beyond the range the model was built for, so those shares are extrapolated. A lower mixing efficiency, Γ = 0.05 (a value Munk and Wunsch (1998) cite for a salt-stratified fjord), cuts every range fourfold (`tidal_mix_g005.py`, seeds 0–3, pre-registered). Europa, Ganymede, Titan, Callisto, Triton, Titania, Oberon, Miranda and Mimas change by at most 2.3 points; Enceladus falls to 36.7% and Umbriel to 55.6%. Ariel (3×10⁻⁸ m² s⁻¹) and Dione (2×10⁻⁷ to 4×10⁻⁷ m² s⁻¹) fall by 20 points, to 24.1% and 31.4%, below their shares with Table 1's range, because their layers seldom merge in time; with the lid test Dione ends at 19.6% (`tidal_mix_g005.log:28–41`). A control at Γ = 0.2 over the same power range stays within 3 points of the values above, so these falls come from the lower Γ.

### 3.4 A stratified lid traps the wall between hands

In the radial model (Eq. 20; `two_zone.log`), with no slow layer or with the slow layer at the bottom, the wall sank to the floor (at 0.67–0.71 times the predicted time with the slow layer at the bottom) and one hand filled the ocean. With a sharp-jointed slow layer on top or in the middle, the wall stopped about 0.08 depth units above the layer's lower edge and did not move again, for mixing ratios of 10 and 100, run to 3 times the predicted time. For this sharp joint, pinning starts at a mixing ratio between 1.02 (the wall passed, 1.378 times slower than without the layer) and 1.05 (it stopped), so any real stratification is far past it. When the mixing instead changes smoothly, the onset falls exactly at Eq. 20 and a wall past it reverses rather than stops (Section 2.4). A bias can push the wall through, but it needs h ≈ 0.20 in model units (`polar_gap.py`); the weak-force bias is g ≤ 2.64×10⁻¹⁶ (ηg ≤ 2.6×10⁻¹⁵), 14–15 orders of magnitude too weak.

The width w of the joint between the layers matters (slow layer on top, mixing ratio 10). With a sharp joint (w = 0.02) the wall stopped at depth 0.323; with w = 0.1 it stopped higher, at 0.137–0.139; with a gradual joint (w = 0.3) it moved up to the ice (depth 0.000), and the deep hand filled the whole ocean. A wall is pinned only where the mixing changes sharply. Counting every stratified draw as a failure (Section 2.5) may therefore be pessimistic for a lid with a gradual base, which would leave one hand set by the deep water.

The measured Enceladus salinity, 5–20 g/kg (Postberg et al., 2009), lies below S\* (Section 2.5). Even its poles are stratified under a steady ice shell (Ames et al., 2025). Counting draws with a stratified top as failures (Section 2.5):

- Enceladus drops from 19–26% to 0% (stratified in 100% of draws; `multiwall.log`, `seedfix.log`). Across the alternative salinity inputs tested it scores 0–21% (Section 3.5; `rerun_base.log`, `seedfix.log`).
- Europa drops from 29–40% to 22–30% (`multiwall.log`; stratified in 23.9% of draws, `rerun_base.log`). Across the alternative salinity inputs tested it ranges over 10–40% (`rerun_base.log`).

**Holes and currents.** A gap in the lid frees the wall only if it is wider than tan θ_c = (h/R)·√(D_side/D_lid). I measured θ_c between 25° and 30° for a 10× slower lid and between 55° and 70° for a 100× slower one. Real polar gaps are far narrower than this. A current crossing the lid can free the wall. For lids 10× and 30× slower, the wall is freed at a Péclet number Pe ≳ 1, where Pe compares the current across the lid with mixing across it. A lid 100× slower stayed pinned up to Pe 80, the strongest current the grid resolves. Early Earth's thermocline gives Pe ~5–10 (Ledwell et al., 1993; Munk, 1966). In a closed-lid run with a fast-mixed surface layer, that is not enough. But Earth's thermocline reaches the surface near the poles, where deep water sinks. With a 20° polar opening and a current sinking at the pole, the wall was freed at Pe 1.2. Earth's share therefore stands because its overturning sinks where its thermocline opens. For Enceladus the contrast between the lid and the convecting interior below it has not been measured; its result stands if the lid mixes at least 100 times more slowly than the interior, the contrast at which a lid stayed pinned up to Pe 80 (a slower lid falls below the grid spacing and was not run; `realistic.py`).

### 3.5 Ranking fourteen ocean worlds

Figure 6 and Table 3 show the result for every world thought to have, or to have had, a liquid-water ocean.

**Table 3.** Success share: the share of draws in which the PVED-favoured hand is selected, with the lid test. Each range runs from the share with the times for walls to rise and sink multiplied by 10 to the share with those times as computed (`multiwall.log`, Section 2.6); the share with no lid is in parentheses for worlds where a lid can form. The Europa and Enceladus rows assume the salinities of Section 3.4 and 20 km of ice for Europa. The last column runs from the share with times ×10 at the deepest ocean shown to the share with times as computed at the shallowest (`rerun_base.log`).

| World | Ocean status | Lid | Success share | Across ocean depths |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ganymede | confirmed | none (~200 MPa of ice) | 34–44% | 21–52% (50–300 km) |
| Europa | confirmed | 24% of draws | 22–30% (29–40%) | — |
| Enceladus | confirmed | 100% of draws | 0% (19–26%) | — |
| Callisto | likely | none | 33–44% | 21–61% (20–300 km) |
| Triton | likely | none | 27–37% | 24–54% (30–200 km) |
| Mimas | likely, < 25 Myr old | 68% of draws | 4–6% (12–17%) | 3–6% (35–55 km) |
| Titan | disputed (Petricca et al., 2025) | none | 26–36% | 18–52% (50–400 km) |
| Pluto | disputed | none | 31–41% | 23–49% (50–200 km) |
| Umbriel, Ariel, Oberon, Titania | possible (Castillo-Rogez et al., 2023) | none | 40–54% | 36–63% (5–50 km) |
| Dione | possible (Beuthe et al., 2016) | 38% of draws | 20–26% (32–42%) | 18–31% (30–100 km) |
| Miranda | past only (Strom et al., 2024) | 67% of draws | 7–9% (21–27%) | 7–9% (100–150 km) |

Two factors set the number. Under a thin ice shell, mildly salty water forms a lid, which pins the wall (Europa, Enceladus, Dione, Mimas, Miranda). Without a lid, the layers must merge, and thinner oceans merge faster. Every lid-free world scores between 26% and 54% (24–39% in the base model). The order is nearly unchanged from the base model: Ganymede and Callisto now rank above Pluto (base model: Pluto 30%, Ganymede 28%), and the order of the three confirmed oceans is unchanged. Depth uncertainty moves a value by up to 30 points (Callisto, times as computed: 61% at 20 km, 31% at 300 km), and the shallower ocean always gives the higher number (`rerun_base.log`).

**Newer measurements.** Juno's microwave radiometer measured Europa's ice shell at 29 ± 10 km (Levin et al., 2025). No lid can form under more than about 23 km of ice. With the shell drawn from that measurement, Europa has a lid in 9% of draws and scores 26–36%, inside Ganymede's own depth spread (21–52%). Higher Enceladus salinity estimates (Kang et al., 2022) give 4–7% (7–30 g/kg) and 13–21% (17–40 g/kg). Enceladus stays below Europa's 22–30% under every lid-forming estimate (0–21%; `rerun_base.log`, `seedfix.log`). With no lid it scores 19–26% (`seedfix.log`), level with Europa under 20 km of ice (22–30%) and below Ganymede (34–44%). Silica nanoparticles suggest that the Enceladus ocean mixes within years (Hsu et al., 2015), which may rule out a stratified top. If so, then in this model its salinity must exceed S\*, and it scores the no-lid value of 19–26%.

Of the three confirmed oceans, Ganymede (34–44%) is the most favourable, then Europa (22–30%), then Enceladus (0%). The Uranian moons score highest of all, but their oceans are model predictions, and the models disagree on present-day thickness (Castillo-Rogez et al., 2023). No percentage here includes the chance that the ocean exists.

![Figure 6 Share of draws in which the PVED-favoured hand is selected, for each world.](../shares3.png)

**Figure 6.** Percentage of 200,000 plausible input draws in which the PVED-favoured hand is selected, for each world in Table 3 (`multiwall.log`, Section 4; Enceladus from `seedfix.log`). Each bar runs from the share with wall rise and sink times multiplied by 10 (left end) to the share with those times as computed (right end). Solid bars include the lid test. Hatched bars show the share if no lid formed, for the five worlds where a lid can form. Colour shows the ocean status. Europa is shown with 20 km of ice; with Juno's measured shell it scores 26–36% (Section 3.5). Drawn by `make_fig_shares3.py`, which asserts every plotted value against its log line.

### 3.6 Closed rocks fade; driven oceans hold

Samples returned from asteroid Bennu, plausibly a fragment of a wet parent body, carry racemic or nearly racemic chiral amino acids (Glavin et al., 2025). This tests the model's assumption that some amplifying chemistry exists. With assumed but literature-bracketed inputs (Glavin et al., 2025; Glavin and Dworkin, 2009), c·τ on Bennu's parent body was 3×10⁸ to 9×10¹¹ M·s. Every draw in the original k₂ range would have finished there. If an amplified excess lasted forever, Bennu would rule out that whole range. With k₂ extended down to 10⁻¹² M⁻¹ s⁻¹, the PVED-favoured hand would be selected in only 1–2% (Enceladus), 3–5% (Europa) and 14% (early Earth) of the draws that keep Bennu racemic (`rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`).

But amplification is a non-equilibrium process: at equilibrium a closed system must end racemic. In a closed, reversible Frank network the excess can rise for a while and then fall back (Blanco et al., 2011; Stich et al., 2016), and a recent prebiotic network behaves the same way (Higgs and Blackmond, 2025). A lasting excess needs a constant supply of free energy (Blackmond, 2004; Plasson et al., 2007). My network does the same when its production steps are made reversible (rates obeying detailed balance) and it is run closed from a 0.1% seed:

- With each reverse rate half its forward rate (s = 1), the excess peaks at 70% and falls below a tenth of its peak after about 7×10³ reaction times.
- With reverse steps 100× slower (s = 0.01), it peaks at 100% and fades after about 2×10⁶.
- The same network kept open (fed and flushed) holds 99.8% for as long as it is driven.

Bennu's parent body was closed, and its water lasted only while short-lived radioactive heat did. If the excess rose and then faded, Bennu stays racemic without ruling out the amplifier. Counting this, the success shares (Enceladus/Europa/Earth, no lid, times ×10 to as computed; `rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`) are:

- 20–27%/29–40%/99.7–99.9% (s = 1) and 21–29%/31–43%/99.7–99.9% (s = 0.01) in the original k₂ range;
- 14–19%/21–29%/74% and 15–21%/23–31%/73% in the extended range.

Reading the excess off the full closed-network curve at the moment the water left, instead of using this step rule, agrees within 3 points. That method also shows that a racemic Bennu is the likely outcome mainly when reverse steps are fast: 97% and 73% of Bennu-like draws freeze racemic with s = 1, against 64% and 43% with s = 0.01 (original and extended k₂ ranges). The 64% depends on how the Bennu concentration and water lifetime are drawn (`quench.log`, Q5, s = 0.01, original k₂ range): it falls to 25.8% if c is the whole feedstock budget, and to 33.2% if c is drawn from Bennu alone with water lasting 10²–10⁷ yr; it is 64.9% with a 12.4% racemic cut-off, 71.6% if c is the free L + D at the peak, and 82.0% if the waste step is reversible. With s = 1 the same variants give 67.2–100.0%. Measured reverse rates and concentrations would therefore determine how much Bennu counts against the model.

This relies on two assumptions. First, an icy-moon ocean must be driven continuously: molecular hydrogen in the Enceladus plume points to ongoing water–rock reactions (Waite et al., 2017), and tidal heating can keep hydrothermal activity going for tens of Myr to Gyr (Choblet et al., 2017). Second, the drive on Bennu's parent body must have stopped while liquid water remained; had both ended together, any excess would have frozen in. The model is therefore compatible with a racemic Bennu only for a closed parent body, a drive that stopped before the water did, and fast reverse steps. I report both the original-range shares (19–26%/29–40%/99.7–99.9%, Section 3.3) and the extended-range shares among Bennu-racemic draws (1–2%/3–5%/14%) as possible. One point stays open: Bennu's isovaline cannot racemize, so the fade rule does not explain why it is racemic.

**A testable prediction.** Without an amplifier, the Enceladus ocean is expected to be racemic, because racemization outpaces production (Steel et al., 2017). A lasting enantiomeric excess in plume amino acids would therefore be a clean sign of amplification in general, not of the PVED, since an amplifier triggered by chance or by a local bias also gives one. What would test the PVED is a consistent sign across independent oceans, or across molecule classes (for example L amino acids with D sugars) in one ocean. A racemic Enceladus plume would be consistent with the model: once its lid is counted, the Enceladus ocean as a whole never has one PVED-set hand (Section 3.4), and a plume can be racemic even when the water below it is not (see below); the hand opposite the calculated PVED found consistently across independent oceans would count against it. Capillary-electrophoresis instruments built for ocean worlds can separate enantiomers in salty samples (Mora et al., 2022). Europa Clipper's Surface Dust Analyser (SUDA), a time-of-flight mass spectrometer (Kempf et al., 2025), cannot make this measurement, because mirror-image molecules have the same mass.

**What a plume would show (`plume_signal.py`).** A plume samples one part of an ocean, and an amplifying network would be only one source of its amino acids among abiotic ones. I estimated both effects for Enceladus under stated assumptions: the water above the lid's density jump and the water below it each choose a hand on their own, with the same gates as the whole ocean and a PVED-set probability that grows with the layer's volume; the upper layer holds 10% of the ocean (scanned 1–30%); the plume comes from that layer; and the measured excess is the network's share of the amino acid times the network's steady purity, reduced by racemization while the water waits to erupt. Under these assumptions the two layers carry opposite hands in only 6.5–11% of the draws in which both amplify, and neither amplifies in 21% of all draws, so a plume usually shows either no excess or the hand of the water below it. This does not contradict the 0% share of Section 3.4, which counts draws in which one hand fills the whole ocean; with a lid that never happens. Racemization matters little for residence times up to 10⁵ years (alanine keeps 95% of its excess) but removes most of it by 10⁷ years (15% kept). Because the network's purity is close to 1 (median 0.99), an excess of 5% needs the network to make at least about 5% of that amino acid, and 1% needs about 1%. With that share unknown (log-uniform from 10⁻⁴ to 1) and a residence time of 10⁵ years, 23% of all draws give a plume excess of at least 5%, and 36% at least 1%.

**The network's share of the amino acid.** No measurement fixes that share: for Enceladus there is only a floor of 0.01 µM for abiotic amino acids (Steel et al., 2017). When I instead build the share from the model's own product concentration and an abiotic background of 0.01–0.14 µM, the upper value derived from the largest abiotic production rate of Steel et al. (2017) (`plume_share.py`), the network makes most of the amino acid in 39% of the draws where it amplifies and under 1% in 31%, and the chance of a plume excess of at least 5% rises to 45% (16–60% for fixed backgrounds from 10 µM down to 1 nM). If the network's feedstock is instead held to the same window as the abiotic background (0.01–0.14 µM; Steel et al., 2017, upper value my derivation), the chance of a plume excess of at least 5% becomes 57% (82% for 1%); with no lower bound on the feedstock it is 15%, and with Steel et al.'s 90 µM biological maximum as the upper bound it is 87% (`plume_feedstock.py`). Because the network's feedstock is an achiral precursor whose concentration no measurement bounds, and because placing feedstock and background in one window makes the answer depend mainly on the assumed product-to-feedstock ratio, these values bracket the plume question rather than settle it: whether abiotic amino acids swamp the network's signal stays open. The success shares of Table 3, which use one feedstock range for all icy moons, are unchanged, and Enceladus with its lid remains at 0%.

**Amplifier or life?** An excess found in only a few amino acids could also be abiotic. Size alone would not tell an amplifier from life (`abiotic_vs_life.py`): the network's own purity is above 0.99 in two thirds of the draws where it works. Two features of the model could: aspartic acid, which racemizes faster, can stay racemic while alanine is pure (7% of the draws where alanine amplifies), and an excess of at least 5% in four amino acids at once falls from 29% to 0.9% of draws if the network makes a different share of each. Outside the model, life is expected to concentrate on a selected set of amino acids rather than the smooth, small-molecule-heavy spread of abiotic chemistry (McKay, 2004; Dorn et al., 2011), and an excess in a non-protein amino acid such as isovaline, as found in meteorites (Glavin and Dworkin, 2009), would point to abiotic chemistry.

**Simulated plume calls.** I simulated the call a plume measurement would make (`life_test.py`, `life_few.py`), using racemization rates for seven protein amino acids (Cohen and Chyba, 2000) plus isovaline, which resists racemization and which I treated as not racemizing at all, with measurement errors of 1–5%. No panel of up to eight amino acids reached 90% correct calls among amplifier, life and plain abiotic chemistry; the best was 63%, mainly because about 40% of amplifier and life cases left every excess below the noise. Whether life can be told from an amplifier depends on how many amino acids the amplifier acts on, which the model does not set. If it acts on only two or three, a rule that calls life when at least four amino acids show an excess of 5% or more is right in 99% of its life calls, and life is identified with confidence in 26% of life cases. If it acts on four or five, the rule is right in 67% of its life calls; if on all seven with life's hand, in 52%, and life is never identified with confidence, because it then looks the same as an amplifier that left isovaline racemic. An isovaline excess helps by marking an amplifier (identified with confidence in 9% of its cases when it acts on all seven), but on its own it separates amplifier from life no better than the protein amino acids do (52% correct for each, where 50% is chance; 61% together). The open question is therefore whether any abiotic process could set the same excess in many amino acids at once.

**One sign is not enough.** The sign in one plume cannot separate the PVED from chance: among detectable plumes the PVED hand appears in 94% of draws, but a chance amplifier would give it in 50%, and one sample cannot tell 94% from 50%. The tests remain those above: the same sign across independent oceans, or the natural pairing of molecule classes in one ocean.

**Sugars (`sugar_pair.py`).** Life pairs L amino acids with D sugars. If the two classes cross the bifurcation coupled, each helping to make the other, their hands are matched, and the pair feels one effective bias that mixes the two PVEDs: g_eff = (√k_as g_a + √k_sa g_s)/√(k_as + k_sa), where g_a and g_s are the amino-acid and sugar PVEDs and k_as and k_sa are the strengths of the links from amino acids to sugars and back. The natural pair is favoured whenever the sugar PVED has the same sign as the amino-acid one, or, if it opposes, whenever the amino-acid-to-sugar link is strong enough (k_as/k_sa > (g_s/g_a)²). Neither ratio has been measured directly. With the sugar sign 50/50, |g_s/g_a| log-uniform over 0.03–3 and k_as/k_sa log-uniform over 0.01–100, the natural pair is favoured in 86% of draws (83–88% for wider and narrower ranges), in 73% even if every sugar PVED opposes, and in all draws if it agrees. The one published calculation in hand, for hydrated D-glyceraldehyde, finds agreement at all 12 conformers, with g_s/g_a of 0.02–0.47 (Zanasi et al., 1999); for ribose the sign depends on the ring's shape (Tranter et al., 1992).

**Measured links (`sugar_measured.py`, `sugar_bands.py`).** Experiments constrain the links themselves, though not cleanly. In the model, the sign of each link decides which sugar hand goes with which amino-acid hand: when both are positive, L amino acids end up with D sugars; when both are negative, with L sugars; and when the two signs differ, no pairing forms (each of the four combinations comes out at 24–26% in simulation). Most experiments point the natural way, but not all. Most L-amino acids make slightly more D- than L-glyceraldehyde, up to 60.7 to 39.3 with L-glutamic acid, while L-proline makes more of the L form (Breslow and Cheng, 2010); L-dipeptides make D-ribose in excess of up to 44%, but lyxose and arabinose rich in the L form (Pizzarello and Weber, 2010). In the other direction, RNA built from D-ribose attaches about four times as much L- as D-amino acid (Tamura and Schimmel, 2004, 2006) and has four to six times more binding sites for L- than for D-histidine (Illangasekare et al., 2010); but when sugars steer an amino-acid synthesis directly, D-ribose and D-deoxyribose favour the unnatural D precursor, and only D-lyxose and mixtures of D-sugars favour the natural L one (Wagner et al., 2017). The model has one net sign for each direction, so it can describe this mixed evidence only on average. Treating each experiment's output excess divided by its input as a gain, and assuming that both classes lose their excess at the same rate, gives a rough, order-of-magnitude ratio k_as/k_sa of 0.46 (90% of draws 0.105–2.02), so the link from sugars to amino acids is usually the stronger one. With that ratio the natural pair is favoured in 84% of draws, and in 68% if every sugar PVED opposes; reasonable choices of which experiments to include, and a factor of three in the ratio, move the first share between 74% and 90% and the second between 49% and 80%. A smaller effective bias lowers the PVED-set shares but never changes their order: with g_eff at one tenth of g_a, the shares without a lid fall by 0.5 points (early Earth) to 7 points (Enceladus). The model therefore explains why the two hands are paired, not which pair: that L amino acids go with D sugars rests on the sign of the amino-acid PVED and on the measured signs of the links, which the experiments, not the model, supply.

### 3.7 Checks against published laboratory amplifiers

These are reanalyses of published numbers (`soai_check.py`, `crystal_check.py`, `viedma_check.py`), not pre-registered tests, except the five marked as pre-registered (`blind_check.py`, `blind_lab.py`, `blind_units.py`, `blind_jang.py`, `blind_solution.py`).

- With no added bias, outcomes split as chance predicts: 46 of 91 spontaneous Soai-reaction runs (Singleton and Vo, 2003; Soai et al., 2003; Soai, 2019) and 37 of 73 crystal-deracemization runs (Miyagawa et al., 2019) gave one hand, both consistent with 50/50.
- Pre-registered (`blind_check.py`; predictions committed before the results of two studies were read): with no added bias, 42 of 72 crystallizers reached L (Deck et al., 2025) and 39 of 68 runs reached one hand (Steendam et al., 2014), both consistent with 50/50 (p = 0.20 and 0.28). The eight crystallizers filled from each shared stock agreed no more often than coin flips would (P = 0.58), so no hidden bias steered these 5 mL runs. All five predictions held. This is weak support: any account in which stray biases are small predicts the same. The AI assistant that drafted the predictions may have seen these papers in training.
- Pre-registered, with graded biases (`blind_lab.py`; predictions committed before a separate reader took the run-level outcomes): the model says the share of runs following a bias rises along one probit curve in the bias times √N. In the Soai reaction with a chiral initiator at nine levels from 0.10% to 96% ee (Hawbaker and Blackmond, 2019, Supplementary Tables 1 and 2), the initiator's hand won every run that ended with a hand at 1.1% ee and above, except one of eleven at 39% ee, and runs split near evenly at 0.10–0.16% ee (17 of 29 followed the initiator). No single probit fits all nine levels (p = 0.014): a curve steep enough for 16 wins in 16 runs at 1.1% and 5% ee makes the one loss at 39% all but impossible. Without that run it fits (p = 0.50), but this check was made after the results were seen and is not counted. Of 49 runs without a chiral initiator, 21 ended with a hand, 16 of them S (p = 0.027 against 50/50): these runs carried a stray bias the model allows but cannot predict. In Viedma grinding at five initial excesses from 10% to 50% (Choi et al., 2024, Fig. 4A), 13 of 57 runs flipped to the minority's hand. One curve fits all five levels (p = 0.90), but only with about 34 deciding units (95% CI 12–76), not the 100–400 starting crystals, so chance matters several times more than a simulation of the grinding mechanism predicts (`viedma_sim.py`, `viedma_big.py`: in the simulation, one curve with the same constant fits every size from 250 to 1,024,000 crystals). Four of the eight predictions held. In both systems a bias had to be about 10⁻³ to 10⁻¹ to steer the outcome, far above the PVED. The AI assistant that drafted the predictions may have seen these papers in training.
- Of 25 side-by-side identical Soai pairs, 12 gave opposite hands. This rejects a shared bias within each pair strong enough to steer (P = 0.018).
- A 5% crystal excess among more than 10⁶ crystals went to 100% of the same hand within 8 h (Viedma, 2005).
- In stirred sodium chlorate crystallization, the whole beaker behaves as about one independent pool (n_eff ≈ 1.0–1.1) when the stirrer breaks crystals, and about 17 when unstirred (Kondepudi et al., 1990; Martin et al., 1996).
- Pre-registered, on the number of deciding units (`blind_units.py`; predictions committed before a separate reader took the outcomes): if a run's hand is the vote of n_eff independent units, ⟨ee²⟩ = 1/n_eff. In 1,1′-binaphthyl crystallizing from its melt at 150 °C (Asakura et al., 2002), stirring cut n_eff from about 270 (unstirred; 7 of 10 runs at ee 0) to 3.2 at 200 rpm and 2.0 at 500 rpm, as in stirred sodium chlorate, and the 23 runs of the stirring series that ended with a hand split 11 S to 12 R. Two predictions failed. Masses grown from a single nucleus were not single-handed (all four at 150 °C had ee 0), and the deciding units far outnumbered the visible crystal aggregates (n_eff at least 88 at 95% confidence, against 2.4 aggregates per run). The paper's own reading is the same: at this supercooling the solid grows as an aggregate of small R and S crystallites, so the unit that decides is a crystallite, not a visible mass. A count of visible crystals therefore bounds the deciding units from neither side: Choi's grinding runs had fewer, these unstirred melts many more. In evaporating sodium chlorate drops (Bak et al., 2023), the share of drops ending with one hand fell with drop volume (1.0, 0.8 and 0.7 at 0.16, 0.87 and 1.7 µL), as the count of extra nuclei predicts, but three points could not test how broad the fall is. Five of nine predictions held, two failed and two could not be scored. The AI assistant that drafted the predictions may have seen these papers in training.
- Pre-registered, a second test of the number of deciding units (`blind_jang.py`; predictions committed before a separate reader took the outcomes): in 1,273 sodium chlorate droplets, about 50–1,000 µm across, that crystallized unstirred in oil carrying an antisolvent from solutions saturated at 35, 40 and 45 °C (Jang et al., 2024), droplets holding both hands were larger than droplets holding one, as the count of extra nuclei predicts (median 560 against 325 µm at 35 °C, p = 2.5×10⁻⁸; 454 against 375 µm at 40 °C, p = 0.013). At 45 °C this held only marginally (449 against 425 µm, p = 0.048): 2 of the 29 mixed droplets were hidden among overlapping markers and were placed by fit, and without them p = 0.092. The hand was chance (559 droplets L only, 610 D only, p = 0.14) and did not depend on size (p = 0.79).
