One prediction failed: a higher supersaturation did not shrink the droplet volume at which mixed droplets become common (exponential fits give 380, 1,150 and 477 nL at 35, 40 and 45 °C). Whether the fall is broad, as the count predicts, or set by a sharp critical size, as the authors propose, could not be scored: no 50 µm size bin had more than 47% of its droplets mixed (5–11% overall). The authors find no mixed droplet below 238–261 µm; the fitted exponentials expect only 0.3–1.0 there, so this does not separate the two readings either (checked after the outcomes were read, not counted). Five of seven predictions held, one failed and one could not be scored. Single-hand droplet sizes are histogram bin centres, because their markers overlap. The AI assistant that drafted the predictions did not recall this paper, but knew from a scout's notes that it reports a critical size.
- Pre-registered, outside crystals (`blind_solution.py`; predictions committed before a separate reader took the outcomes): in a Langmuir monolayer whose domains take a hand from a chiral dopant and from the sense of stirring (Petit-Garrido et al., 2012), the two biases added as one summed bias. The dopant moved the stirring rate at which the hand switches (probit fits: +222 rpm with the R dopant, −211 rpm with S, −26 rpm with the racemic mix) without changing how sharply it switches (steepness within a factor 1.35), and the R and S curves were mirror images. In the uncatalysed aldol reaction run with no chiral additive (Mauksch et al., 2010), the 13 runs above the authors' 2% threshold split 8 S to 5 R, and the excess fell with volume (RMS ee 4.8%, 7.5% and 2.3% at 0.15, 0.3 and 2.5 mL; log–log slope −0.33, against a prediction of above −0.25). This cannot show that each molecule votes: all six 2.5 mL runs ended R and 9 of the 10 smaller runs S (seen after the outcomes were read), and reactant precipitated only in the smaller runs. In 116 printed runs of the Soai reaction started with three achiral amines (Suzuki et al., 2010; Table 1 lists run 26 twice), 59 ended S and the amines did not differ (P = 0.93), but in table order the hand changed between consecutive runs more often than chance (74 changes against about 58, P = 0.003), and R runs ended at higher ee (median 19% against 11%, p = 0.016), from one amine (19% against 7%; another went the other way). The paper does not say whether its run numbers follow time order. Eight of eleven predictions held. Whether a reaction in solution reaches the best case of Eq. 13 is still untested. The AI assistant that drafted the predictions may have seen these papers in training.

So both a self-copying step and transport are needed, as the model assumes. These checks support the counting rule (a bias controls the outcome only when it exceeds the chance excess of about 1/√N) in vessels holding 10⁶–10¹² crystals or molecules, where about 1–300 units decide, a number set by copying and transport rather than by that count. They do not test the weak force, the 10³¹–10³⁴-molecule threshold or any ocean. Lab tests of the Soai reaction also need biases far larger than the PVED to set the outcome (Hawbaker and Blackmond, 2019), as expected for flask-sized volumes.

### 3.8 Stability of the selected hand

A pool that has passed the transition does not drift (Table 2: the favoured share held at 0.730 for 100× the selection window). It flips only by a rare noise-driven jump, at Kramers' rate (√2/2π)·e^(−N/2) per unit time 1/k (ε = 1/N). At least one flip is expected within the reaction time only if (√2/2π)·e^(−N/2)·kτ ≥ 1, that is N ≤ 2 ln(0.225 kτ): N ≈ 2 at kτ = 10, 20 at kτ = 10⁵, and 57 at the largest kτ sampled (about 10¹³). Only pools of order tens of molecules can flip; a droplet, pond or ocean never does.

### 3.9 Models of the planned arms at measured rates

Where rates are published, the arms of the wet-lab search (Section 5) can be modelled before they are run. Models of arms E and G at measured rates predict that neither breaks symmetry yet, with one reading of one arm G measurement as the exception, which the gel fit below disfavours. In arm E, uncatalysed breakdown of alanine outruns the catalysed path (Yu et al., 2024): at 1–5 mM the catalysed rate would have to be about 700–3700 times higher before the racemic state is lost.

**Arm G's templated step.** Joyce et al.'s (1984) yields of oligomers of four or more residues after 7 days at 0 °C give an addition rate constant of at least 1.7×10⁻⁵ M⁻¹ s⁻¹ with all-D monomer and 1.7×10⁻⁶ with racemic monomer (`joyce_r5.py`, predictions written before the paper was read), above the rate an ocean needs (3×10⁻⁹ M⁻¹ s⁻¹ at 1 µM over 10⁸ yr; Section 5) and, for all-D monomer, the rate a pond needs (3×10⁻⁶ at 1 mM over 100 yr). These are rates of a step that only inhibits the other hand, not of self-copying.

**A peptide model (`aped.py`).** A model of N-carboxyanhydride peptides with activation, polymerization, epimerization and depolymerization, in its reversible form with monomer racemization (Plasson and Bersini, 2009), re-implemented from its arXiv version (`aped.py`, which also holds the irreversible model below), loses the racemic state only between 0.0105 and 2.11 M of activation fuel at its base parameters, only with Plasson's rate signs (heterochiral steps disfavoured: 5 of 16 such sets have a threshold, 0 of 16 sets with heterochiral-favouring signs), and only at about 1 M or more of total residues (none at 0.5 M), so it shows the slow tip-over that Eq. 11 assumes (the racemic state stable at low drive and lost above a threshold) only in part, and only above the 1 mM to 0.1 M of the planned arms. The lower drive threshold comes from the racemization step. A separate re-implementation of the original irreversible model (Plasson et al., 2004) from its full text, which has no racemization, has no lower drive threshold: at its Fig. 5 rates and 1 M of residues the racemic state is lost at every activation rate from zero up to an upper edge, and at the published activation rate it is lost once total residues exceed 66.9 mM, with an e-folding time of about 50 years at 1 M, so there the threshold is in concentration rather than drive. In the reversible model the racemic state is stable at every concentration of the three settings (10⁻⁶ to 0.1 M), so a seeded excess decays.

**Arm G: the ratio r.** In arm G (rates from Walton and Szostak, 2017), the racemic state is lost only if adding a monomer of the wrong hand is faster than adding one of the right hand, with the ratio r between 1 and 1.9 (washout sets the upper limit). Essex et al. (2026) give r = 0.059 for monomer alone, 0.40 (95% interval 0.22–0.98) for the bridged pathway from their rate constants and formation rates, and 0.50 for a monomer bridged to a D-trimer. Their direct competition between hands gives 0.49 if the measured reduction counts products past +2 and 1.14 (per point 1.06–1.23) if it counts those past +1, and the preprint does not say which. Fitting the +1 band share of the seven competition lanes of the SI gel without its pure-D control (Essex et al., 2026, SI Fig. S8; `fig_s8_r_fit.py`) gives r = 0.56 (95% interval 0.45–0.68; at most 0.79 across band-reading variants), and r = 1 is rejected unless fewer than 0.10 of the D-ended primers stall at +1 anyway (the 90% ee lane suggests about 0.18). This is an upper bound from one gel read from a figure; it agrees with 0.40 and 0.49 and disfavours 1.14. A shared achiral activator does not change this: with template growth exponential or parabolic the racemic state is never lost at r = 0.4–0.6 (0 of 36 cases across feeds and activator levels), and only a hyperbolic control loses it, on a template-rich branch that a feed-filled reactor does not reach.

**Arm G: what it passes on.** Every r ≤ 0.98 leaves the racemic state stable at every feed, so arm G is expected only to pass on an excess in its feed (a +20% feed reaches a plateau of 0.67 at r = 0.40), as is arm E; r = 1.14, a reading the gel fit disfavours, would put the threshold feed at 10.1 mM. The excess has to be in the feed: in the arm G model an excess placed in the templates of a racemic feed decays by a factor of 0.979 to 0.9995 per cycle and is lost in every case tested (54 of 54; cycles of 1 h or 24 h, 100 yr to 10⁸ yr), whereas a feed excess of 1% is copied to 3.4% to 79% at 20 mM. A racemic monomer pool slows copying (onset feed 6.3 to 7.6 mM against 2.9 mM for a pure pool; yield at 20 mM 44% to 61% of a pure pool) but does not stop it or erase a feed excess; at the 0.1 mM and 1 µM of the lake and ocean settings the model does not copy for either pool (`arm_g_r6.py`).

**Arm E passes on a feed excess.** At measured rates arm E's network passes a feed excess through with a gain of at most 2.45, so a 1% output needs a feed excess of 0.2–0.4%, against the 5.8 × 10⁻¹² needed in the pond, and an excess seeded into the pool is followed only down to about 44 times that need (pond, lake); at 1.5 times the multiplier that makes its racemic state unstable, a feed excess is followed far below the need and a seeded one only down to 1.25–1.85 times it (`arm_e_r6.py`). If either arm crosses its threshold, both models predict the slow tip-over of Eq. 11, with no hysteresis.

**No measured amplifier is sensitive enough.** Neither arm has the sensitivity the model asks for, an excess seeded into the pool followed down to the size the pool needs; passing on an excess in the feed does not count, because a racemic state that stays stable passes a feed excess through without amplifying it. No published amplifier meets this rule either (`lit_r6.py`, predictions written before the papers were re-read). A hand decided among N units cannot follow a seed smaller than about 2/√N, so following the pond's 5.8 × 10⁻¹² takes about 10²³ deciding molecules. The Soai reaction is decided by at most about 3.6 × 10⁹ molecules (`soai_check.py`), and grinding (Viedma, 2005) and magnetite crystallization (Ozturk et al., 2023b) by crystals or a surface (Section 3.7); on magnetite the hand is set by the surface's magnetization.

## 4 Discussion and limitations

The size rule restates the balance of Kondepudi and Nelson (1985), who already argued that a weak-current bias needs a large system and a long time, as a count of molecules in one mixed pool against which any setting can be checked; what is new is the anisotropic stretching (Eq. 15), wall pinning by stratification and wall reversal past its onset (Eq. 20), and the planetary Monte Carlo. Ponds, pores and diffusion-linked vent mounds fall short; oceans clear the size bar, and then the question is how they mix. A PVED-selected hand needs a large, slow, continuously driven reactor, not a flask, a pond or a closed rock.

This size rule holds only if the whole body of water passes through the transition together. If the drive instead crosses the water as a slow front, the hand is set in a layer of thickness ℓ_A = 2.24(Dvτ/k)^(1/3) at the front and copied as the front advances, whatever the size of the body (Section 2.3; Kibble and Volovik, 1997; Dziarmaga et al., 1999). The molecules that count are then those of that layer, and a larger ocean adds none. The same holds for a flat front in 2D and 3D (Section 2.3). Whether an ocean's drive ramps everywhere at once or as a front is not known for any world. If the drive is supplied locally, at vents, and spread by horizontal mixing, the crossing time differs across an ocean of width L by about the mixing time (L/A)²/D_h (A = 5.5, Eq. 17), so the front speed in units of V₀ is u = A²ξ/L, where ξ = √(D_h/k)(kτ)^(1/4). Under this assumption 2% (Miranda) to 17% (Ganymede) of draws per world cross as a front (u < 3; early Earth 2.8%), and in every draw of every world mixing spreads the drive faster than it changes. The front draws are the fast-reaction draws, whose Δ exceeds the threshold by a factor of about 5,000 to 400,000 (found after the run). Giving them the front layer's N in place of the ocean's changes no world's P_pved by more than 0.01 points, and by at most 0.08 points with a front 5.5 times slower (`front2d.py`). A drive set globally, such as cooling through the ice shell, is uniform. A drive confined to one stagnant region, or one slower than mixing, is outside this test.

A local bias that only Earth has does not strengthen the effect of the PVED. Magnetite magnetized by a planetary field can give an RNA precursor a large excess (Ozturk et al., 2023a, 2023b). But the field points opposite ways in the two hemispheres, and its polarity at any time is random. In my early-Earth runs it makes the outcome a coin flip (L 47%, D 46%, no single hand 7%). The lead of the PVED-favoured hand is halved once more than 1.6×10⁻¹⁵ of the molecules that set the outcome form on magnetized magnetite, and the outcome is a coin flip once that share exceeds 3×10⁻¹³ (`rerun_base.log`). For Europa and Enceladus, the PVED remains the controlling bias only if less than about 2.6–3.3×10⁻¹⁵ of the chemistry in the selection window occurs on such surfaces (`rerun_base.log`, Enceladus from `seedfix.log`).

**How a Europa lander could test this.** The Europa Lander mission concept (Hand et al., 2022) would sample surface ice. Radiation alters organic molecules there to depths of about 1 cm at mid- to high latitudes and 10–20 cm in the radiation lenses centred on the leading and trailing hemispheres (Nordheim et al., 2018), so the sample must come from below that depth. A chiral separation of its amino acids, for example by capillary electrophoresis (Mora et al., 2022), has three possible outcomes. (i) No excess. In the multiwall column this is the most likely result for Europa (66% with a lid, 56% without), so a racemic sample counts only weakly against the PVED. With mixing set by the tidal energy budget (Section 3.3), no excess falls to 37% with a lid and 17% without, and a racemic sample would count more strongly. (ii) An excess of the PVED-favoured hand. (iii) An excess of the other hand. A chance-set hand is either hand equally often, so among Europa draws that end with one hand, the PVED-favoured hand appears in (30.1 + 3.7/2)/(30.1 + 3.7) = 95% (the same with no lid), against 50% if chance alone set it (98% with the energy-budget mixing). Outcome (ii) at one world therefore shifts the odds toward the PVED by a factor of about 1.9, and the same hand at two independent worlds by about 3.6; outcome (iii) counts against it. Two complications apply. First, the sign of the amino-acid PVED in water is not settled (Section 2.1), so "PVED-favoured" can only mean the sign calculated at the time of the measurement. Second, carbonaceous meteorites carry a large L excess of isovaline (18.5 ± 2.6% in Murchison), amplified on their parent bodies from a small initial asymmetry (Glavin and Dworkin, 2009). Delivered material of this kind would be a second bias, as magnetite is for Earth. Measuring several amino acids, and molecule classes whose calculated PVED signs differ (Section 3.6), separates the two: the PVED gives each molecule its own calculated sign, while a delivered excess carries the pattern of its source. Samples from more than one site also test the layer results directly: if the sampled ice comes from the ocean, an ocean that ended with one hand gives the same hand at every site.

The main limitations are as follows.

- **All results are order-of-magnitude estimates.** The factor F ≈ 0.7 is checked for only two reaction schemes. Another scheme could give a smaller F.
- **Mixing is the key unknown.** Vertical mixing in icy-moon oceans is a model input, uncertain across 10⁻⁹–10⁻³ m² s⁻¹ (Zeng and Jansen, 2021, 2024; Kang et al., 2022). How it varies with depth sets which layer's hand fills the ocean: the results assume it rises with depth (assumption 7); if it is as likely to fall, each moon loses up to 7 points (Section 3.3); and the rule that the layer at the onset fills the ocean holds at ocean scale only when that layer is at least three wall widths thick, which about half of the deciding layers are not; a handoff rule tested on new runs agrees with them in 96.5% of runs, above the pre-registered 95%, except where every layer is thinner than one wall width (Section 3.2). Independent patches do not add up to exactly one pool (c = 0.92 in 1D at about 80 patches and 0.98 in 2D; about 0.98 in 3D from theory, not simulated), and the sampling uses f = 1, which overstates Δ by about 2% in 3D (Sections 2.3, 3.2); using c = 0.88, the value at 10–40 patches, instead moves any share by at most 0.6 points (`rerun_base.log`). The mixing model is simple eddy diffusion, with one idealized overturning cell, not a full circulation model; how eddies and the sphere slow it is in the next two bullets. The energy budget (Section 2.6) puts interior mixing above the sampled range for the larger moons and raises most shares to 73–81% (Section 3.3). It assumes the tidal power is spent throughout the ocean and that every world has Europa's interior density contrast (Ashkenazy and Tziperman, 2021); six moons rest on a single source of tidal power, and Pluto has none, so it keeps its full range. Earth's deep stratification is renewed by sinking cold water, which is unlikely in icy-moon oceans (F. Nimmo, pers. comm.); a weaker contrast would raise interior mixing, while power spent in boundary layers at the ice or the seafloor (Jansen et al., 2023) would lower it. The lid under the ice is set by the heat flux itself and does not depend on this.
- **Eddies larger than the wall width slow wall removal.** Treating eddies as diffusion assumes the wall width exceeds the eddy size (Section 2.3); for a Rhines-scale eddy of a 0.02 m s⁻¹ flow this fails in 16–39% of the sampled draws (`h14_eddy.log:26–40`). A 2D test with explicit eddies (Section 2.3) supports the outcome rule but shows that such eddies leave more walls at the transition and clear them several times more slowly than diffusion at the same D_eff would. The slowdown was measured in 2D at eddy sizes of 3.6, 10 and 30 wall widths, with one eddy per box and molecular diffusion at 1/8 to 1/4096 of the total (`h14f_advect.log:51-64`). At 10 wall widths the median clearing time was 5.8 to 28.9 times Eq. 17's, growing with the ratio of total to molecular diffusivity as a power of 0.40 up to 1/64 and 0.23 to 0.24 over the next two doublings (`h14e_advect.log:68-70`), and 0.17 (0.12 to 0.18) over the four doublings to 1/4096; a logarithmic law fitted before the 1/4096 run predicted that point (27.9 against 28.9, 95% range 25.3 to 29.7; `h14f_advect.log:73`, `:111`). The real ratio is about 10⁹ and was not reached; extrapolated over the remaining 18 doublings, the slowdown there is 29 if growth stops, 75 for a logarithmic law and 131 to 276 for a power law with the exponent measured over the last four doublings (`h14f_advect.log:90-96`). The exponent has fallen over nine doublings, as the logarithmic law requires; that it keeps falling over the next 18 is not shown. On a sphere a shallow ocean still coarsens to a single domain in the same model (case i; 664 of 664 runs, and 12 of 12 in a thin 3D shell), but the endgame takes a median 7 times longer than the sweep's sideways term (90th percentile 17), which the sweep does not include (Section 3.2). None of this was tested in 3D, and the sphere factor was measured without eddies.
- **A slower coarsening decides a draw only past its margin.** The times ×10 range of Table 3 lengthens the times for walls to rise and sink between layers, not the sideways coarsening time of Eq. 17, so it does not cover the slowdown of the previous bullet. Making the sideways coarsening time 4 or 10 times longer in every draw, a worst case, changes no share by more than 0.1 point (`coarsen_slow.py`, `coarsen_slow.log`). Sideways coarsening takes at most 7% of the ocean's age in any draw (`h13_sphere_shares.log:2-16`), so a slowdown decides a draw only when it exceeds that draw's margin, as small as 14 for Ganymede and as large as 2400 for early Earth's ocean (`h13h14_extreme.log:3-17`). The sphere factor alone moves no share by more than 0.16 points (0.32 at 23; `h13_sphere_shares.log:38-39`). Applying the sphere factor of 17 times the measured eddy slowdowns to every draw, a worst case that assumes the two multiply, gives 34 to 307, and no share moves by more than 2.7 points up to 100 or 6.3 points at 300 (`h13h14_combined.log`); with the extrapolated eddy slowdowns of the previous bullet the product is about 490 to 4.7 × 10³. Sideways eddy mixing (Zhang et al., 2024) moves a share by a point or more only if it slows horizontal coarsening by more than about 40 times (`h13h14_combined_cross.log`). Slowing every draw by 3000 takes 17.1 points from Ganymede and 16.1 from Callisto, by 10,000 takes 22.9 and 21.9, and by 100,000 takes 38.8 from early Earth's ocean (`h13h14_extreme.log:39-40,124-127`). Slowing every draw by up to 1000 moves early Earth by 0.0025 points, and it stays at 99.9% up to about 2600; it is 99.8% from about 2800, below 95% by 9000, 61% at 100,000 and 17% at 1,000,000 (`h13h14_extreme.log:216-223`; `h13h14_extreme_locate.log:246-259`). Its 99.9% therefore holds at the real ratio under the logarithmic law (a factor of 1270), if growth stops (490), and under a power law with an exponent below about 0.13 (`h14f_advect.log:91`); a power law that holds the measured 0.17 from 1/4096 on gives 4.0 × 10³ and a share between 98.9 and 99.4% (`h13h14_extreme_locate.log:253-254`). At 1.8 × 10⁸ and above no draw of any world finishes in time and every share is 0 (`h13h14_extreme_locate.log:271`).
- **Melting of the ice shell can make a lid that the salinity rule misses.** Fresh meltwater can form a stable layer under the ice on Europa, Ganymede and Enceladus alike (see also Ashkenazy and Tziperman, 2021), and Section 2.5 does not include it. If such a lid forms in a fraction p of draws, independently of the other inputs, every share is multiplied by 1 − p (within 0.13 points), so the order of the worlds does not change. Ganymede falls below Europa only if it has a melt lid in more than 32–34% of draws while Europa has none, or more than 18–21% against Europa with the Juno shell (`lid_melt.py`, `lid_melt.log`). I have no estimate of p for any world. The one published constraint is on Europa: in both coupled thermal–orbital models of Hussmann and Spohn (2004) its ice shell is several tens of km thick today and thickening, so it is freezing at its base and not melting, and p = 0 for Europa is the published case and the one used in this test; Ganymede, which that work does not model, has no published p, so the break-even above stays the test. The order of the worlds holds by construction against any effect that hits every world equally; the sideways slowdown does not hit worlds equally (it scales with L²/D_h): at a factor of 10,000 Ganymede, Callisto and Titan fall from ranks 6, 7 and 12 to 12, 11 and 14 in the no-lid column while the top five are unchanged as a set, and early Earth's ocean stays first up to 1,000,000 (`h13h14_extreme.log:155-157,167`); only effects that differ between worlds test it. With the lid test, Ganymede stays above Europa at both ends of its range up to a factor of 30,000 and falls just below at 100,000; inside the extrapolated range of 490 to 4.7 × 10³ (previous bullet) their ranges overlap (Ganymede 23.4–32.2% and Europa 18.5–25.7% at 1000; 19.4–27.0% and 15.8–22.3% at 3000; `h13h14_extreme.log:21–24`). Salt layers deep in Ganymede's ocean (below) would put it at 17–22%, under Europa's 22–30%, and so would a melt lid much likelier on Ganymede than on Europa.
- **The time τ is set to the ocean's age.** This is the most favourable case.
- **The racemization test is weaker than the network needs.** The main runs ask only that k₂a exceed 2k_rac (Section 2.6), and apply this to every draw except the reversed-wall ones. For the network of Eq. 2 the threshold is about 5.7 times higher (about 22 times for a purity of 0.9), and it varies from 3 to 26 with constants I did not sample (`kcheck.log:11–17`). Applying the weaker test to every draw lowers the shares by up to 2.4 points (Ganymede; `kcheck.log:142`). Replacing it with the network's own threshold (5.7 times) lowers the no-lid shares by 0.3 to 5.6 points for the icy worlds (Ganymede 44.1 to 38.5, Europa 39.6 to 34.9, Enceladus 26.4 to 25.4) and by less than 0.1 for early Earth; the order of the worlds and the lid result do not change (`kcheck.log:62–77, 144–145`). With it, the ranges of the abstract would read Ganymede 29.4–38.5%, Europa 19.3–26.5% with its lid, Enceladus 18.6–25.4% without one, and early Earth at least 67.0% (`kcheck.log:151`). I have not replaced the numbers of the abstract, Table 3 and Section 3.3; they come from the runs with the weaker test, and this is a sensitivity result.
- **Not modelled:** salt layers deep in large oceans (the "club sandwich" of Vance et al. (2014); if they pinned Ganymede half the time, it would drop from 34–44% to 17–22%; `rerun_base.log`); ammonia, which is likely in outer-Solar-System oceans but missing from TEOS-10; and temperature differences between the moons, which are all held at 273 K. For early Earth alone, an ocean drawn between 273 and 343 K, spanning temperate (Blake et al., 2010) to hot (Knauth and Lowe, 2003; Robert and Chaussidon, 2006) estimates, speeds racemization and lowers the share from 99.9% to 97.5% for alanine and 94.5% for aspartic acid, or to 93.9% and 86.4% if the racemization test is applied to every draw (`rerun_base.log`).
- **The bias itself is uncertain.** Its sign for amino acids in water is unsettled: calculations for the zwitterion in water change sign with its conformation and with the method, and none of the high-level ones settles it (Berger and Quack, 2000; MacDermott et al., 2009; Quack et al., 2022). The sign decides which pair of hands the model favours, and the sugar PVED may point the "wrong" way (Quack et al., 2022). If amino acids and sugars are coupled, an opposing sugar PVED still leaves the natural pair favoured in 73% of draws (Section 3.6), but which pair life uses still rests on the amino-acid sign.
- **The model stops once one hand fills the mixed volume.** Whether that hand then lasts is not part of the main model. Avetisov (1999) argued that a pool made pure by a weak chiral field could not stay pure as enantiospecific biochemistry arose, and that purity from spontaneous symmetry breaking needs catalysts about as selective as enzymes. A separate deterministic calculation (`persist.py`, with the constants of Section 2.2.1 and Table 1 and predictions written before the run) tests part of this. Without autocatalysis a pure pool loses its excess: for alanine at 273 K half in 1.2 Myr and 99% in 8.0 Myr (aspartic acid 0.40 and 2.7 Myr; `persist.log:2, 5`). The ocean age of Table 1 is shorter than 8.0 Myr for 30.1% of the Enceladus range (14.2% for aspartic acid) and for none of the Europa and early-Earth range (`persist.log:9, 12`), so with nothing to restore it a pure hand would not last across most of the range; the weak field alone holds an excess of at most 4.3×10⁻⁶ (`persist.log:16–20`). With autocatalysis and the feed running, the pure state is stable and the mixed state unstable only if racemization is below 0.087 of the outflow rate (`persist.log:24–38`), that is, only if k₂a exceeds 2k_rac by a factor of about 5.7 (about 22 for a purity above 90%; Section 2.6), not the factor of 1 that the main runs test. Of the Table 1 draws for alanine at 273 K, 73.6–74.0% (icy worlds) and 100.00% (early Earth) pass the first factor, and 67.2–67.5% and 99.2% the second (`kcheck.log:44–58`). If copying makes the wrong hand a fraction (1 − V)/2 of the time (Avetisov's scheme 7), a pure state needs V ≥ 0.85, and a purity above 90% needs V ≥ 0.96 (0.9975 above 99%) with no racemization (`persist.log:53`): nearly enantioselective catalysts, as Avetisov required. A consumer that uses the majority hand faster than the other leaves the pure state in place if it acts only on a pure pool (gated, 40 or 130 links; `persist.log:97–113`), but a weak gate (10 links) fails at strengths of 3 and above (`persist.log:93–95`). If it acts regardless of purity and is strong enough, it flips the pool to the other hand: the critical selectivity is 5.9 at a consumer strength of 0.1 of the decay rate and 1.15 at 0.3, and at 1 or more the excess is lost even without selectivity (`persist.log:81–86`). This supports Avetisov's point that persistence needs strong catalysis. It is a deterministic test at one parameter set (a₀ = 1.6, k = k_i = k_d = f = 1, k₀ = 10⁻³), with no noise, no measured enzyme selectivity and no case with a supporting field, so it does not test his conclusion that physical chiral factors cannot matter.
- **No measured prebiotic reaction does this yet.** The Soai reaction amplifies but is not prebiotic. The closest prebiotic candidates, peptide ligations that amplify an excess (Deng et al., 2024) and a network that adds amino-acid synthesis and breaks symmetry on its own (Higgs and Blackmond, 2025), give rates only as ratios, so k₂ is still swept. A rough rate from a peptide-catalysed transamination that makes enantioenriched alanine (Yu et al., 2024) lies inside the swept range.

The most robust result does not rest on the ranking. In every ocean, chance sets the hand in at most 15% of plausible input sets, and among draws that end with one hand, the PVED sets it in 73–100% (Section 3.3). Where the bias fails, the usual outcome is no single hand, not a hand set by chance.

The testable result is a ranking, not an absolute probability. A deep ocean with no lid (Ganymede) or a thick-shelled Europa is a better place than Enceladus to look for a hand selected by the weak force.

## 5 Planned experiment

No known prebiotic reaction meets all the needs of the model: self-copying, mutual suppression, prebiotic plausibility and a biologically relevant product. I have therefore pre-registered a wet-lab search for one. It has **not yet been run**. The hypothesis is that at least one prebiotic, water-based, peptide-forming system will show seed-following growth of enantiomeric excess (ee). A positive result would show amplification, not PVED selection (Section 3.6).

The smallest detectable rate is k₂,min = ln(1 + 3√2 σ/e₀)/(cT). Here σ is the standard error of the mean ee of one condition (the per-vial ee error divided by √n for n vials), e₀ the seed excess, c the concentration and T the run length. At realistic concentrations, the rate needed for a pond is 3×10⁻⁶ M⁻¹ s⁻¹ (1 mM, 100 yr), and for an ocean 3×10⁻⁹ (1 µM, 10⁸ yr). No lab run can reach the ocean value at natural concentration. The design therefore has two arms.

1. **Realistic arm:** 1 mM, a 20% seed, 3 years, and 9 independent vials per condition at 2.6% ee error per vial (Glavin and Dworkin, 2009). This reaches 1.8×10⁻⁶ M⁻¹ s⁻¹, below the pond value. With only 3 vials it would reach 2.9×10⁻⁶.
2. **Accelerated arm:** 0.01 M and 0.1 M, stated plainly as not natural. It looks for any amplifier and measures how the rate of ee growth scales with concentration.

Seven chemistries are tested:

- (A) amino acids with carbonyl sulfide (Leman et al., 2004);
- (B) amino acids with hydroxy acids under wet–dry cycling (Forsythe et al., 2015);
- (C) cysteine peptides that catalyse their own ligation (Foden et al., 2020);
- (D) an RNA precursor on magnetite (Ozturk et al., 2023b);
- (E) peptide-catalysed transamination coupled to ligation, run fed rather than closed (Yu et al., 2024; Deng et al., 2024; Higgs and Blackmond, 2025);
- (F) an RNA precursor made from sugar precursors with chiral amino-acid or dipeptide catalysis, then crystallized, run fed (Breslow and Cheng, 2010; Hein et al., 2011; Ozturk and Sasselov, 2025);
- (G) nonenzymatic copying of short RNA primers with 2-aminoimidazole-activated nucleotides, fed fresh monomer and temperature-cycled to separate strands (Joyce et al., 1984; Essex et al., 2026; Kim et al., 2025).

Only some arms can carry a PVED-selected hand. A bias the size of the PVED can steer only a decision made by a huge well-mixed pool, and in the crystallizations of Section 3.7 only about 1–300 units decided (Section 2.2.4). Arm D and the crystallization step of arm F can therefore amplify, but the PVED cannot steer them; a positive result there shows amplification only. In arms C, E and G the decision is made molecule by molecule in solution, and in A and B too if their peptides stay dissolved. Arm G has the clearest evidence of mutual suppression in solution: L-nucleotides inhibit the template-directed synthesis of D-oligonucleotides (Joyce et al., 1984), and an L-nucleotide at the 3′ end of a D-RNA primer ends copying (Essex et al., 2026, a preprint). Its templated step is fast enough (Section 3.9), but that step only inhibits the other hand. Self-copying has not been shown there, so arm G asks whether repeated copying cycles make a seeded excess grow. It forms no peptides, so it tests the hypothesis only in its broader form. Arms E and G are also run at three or more feed rates, to see whether the racemic state is stable at low feed and lost above a threshold, the slow tip-over that Eq. 11 assumes; no system is yet known to do this. Models of arms E and G at measured rates predict that neither breaks symmetry yet, and that each would only pass on an excess already in its feed (Section 3.9). For arm G the deciding ratio r (Section 3.9) can be measured directly, so it is measured first; the gel fit favours r < 1, and the direct measurement still decides.

Of the runs that test whether a seeded excess grows, arms E and C are the priority. The arm E model predicts no symmetry breaking at the rates measured so far, but E has a published model in which it breaks symmetry on its own (Higgs and Blackmond, 2025), and C's peptides catalyse their own ligation (Foden et al., 2020); measuring each arm's own rates is the point. A Viedma grinding positive control (Viedma, 2005) checks that the pipeline can detect real amplification. Each arm gets +L, +D (mirror control) and racemic seeds, plus negative controls (no activator, sterile-filtered, seed alone). Analysts are blinded. An arm counts as an amplifier only if all of the following hold:

- both seeded sets rise by more than the detection threshold;
- the L- and D-seeded sets change by equal and opposite amounts;
- the racemic vials stay at zero;
- the controls stay flat.

The D-seeded mirror arm is the main guard against contamination by biological L-amino acids.

## 6 Conclusions

- For the PVED, rather than chance, to control the outcome, at least about 5×10³⁰ to 2×10³⁴ molecules must react together as one well-mixed pool over the amino-acid PVEDs sampled (0.1–6.2 aeV). Ponds and rock pores fall short at every value sampled, and a well-mixed lake suffices only at the top of the range. If the PVED maps onto the rate bias and the whole volume passes through the transition together rather than as a slow front, a weak-force-selected hand needed a large-lake- to ocean-scale mixed volume.
- If vertical mixing rises with depth, the PVED-favoured hand is selected, before the lid test, in at least 67% of plausible early-Earth cases when cases with several walls count as no single hand (89–99.9% when the rule that the deepest layer's hand fills the ocean decides them, and about 98% when a thin deciding layer hands the decision to the layer above), 19–26% for Enceladus and 29–40% for Europa (22–26% and 30–35% if mixing is as likely to fall with depth in the moons). Vertical mixing, and how it varies with depth, is the main unknown. In every ocean, chance sets the hand in at most 15% of input sets, and most single-hand outcomes follow the PVED.
- A stratified lid under thin ice traps the wall between hands. Among confirmed oceans, Ganymede scores highest (34–44%), then Europa (22–30%), and Enceladus lowest (0% when a lid forms; 0–21% across salinity estimates).
- An amplified excess fades in a closed rock and holds in a driven ocean. This is compatible with a racemic Bennu if its parent body was closed, its drive stopped while water remained and reverse steps were fast, with one open point (Bennu's isovaline). A lasting excess in a driven ocean such as that of Enceladus would test amplification; a consistent sign across independent oceans or molecule classes would test the PVED.
- The missing piece is a prebiotic amplifier. A bench-top search sized for realistic concentrations is planned.

## Data availability

No new experimental data were generated. All model outputs can be regenerated from the code below. Literature values used as inputs are cited in the text. Values read from published tables and figures (Sections 3.7, 3.9 and 5) are entered in the scripts that use them, each with its source table or figure; band intensities read from the SI gel of Essex et al. (2026) are in `fig_s8_bands.log`.

## Code availability

All code is public at https://github.com/helloamonkey742-code/chirality-project under the MIT licence. It needs Python with numpy, scipy, matplotlib and numba; `life_test.py` and `life_few.py` also need scikit-learn, and `abiotic_vs_life.py` needs RDKit (tested versions in `requirements.txt`). `./run_all.sh` runs a fast subset (about 2 minutes), `./run_all.sh --full` adds the slow 3D and network runs (about 50 minutes), and `./run_all.sh --paper` reruns the scripts behind the tables, figures and the other results in the table below (about 70 minutes). The longest parameter sweeps (`front2d.py` 2D and 3D sections, `gradient_ramp.py`, `kz_patches.py`, `deephand_scale.py` section 1) and the grid-resolution check `shell_res.py` take hours, and `verify_sstar.py` needs the gsw package; these, with `realistic.py` section 5 and `sphere_layers.py` section 2b, are run by hand with the arguments in each script's docstring. Most of the older scripts assert their checks and stop with an error if one fails. Predictions written before a run (Section 2.7) do not stop it: some scripts print each one as HELD or FAILED, others print the numbers it is judged on, and each docstring's RESULTS block lists every failed prediction. `quench.py` collects its checks and exits with an error at the end; `verify_seeds.py` prints ok or ASSERT FAILED for each seed. `field_flips.py`, `field_walls.py`, `realistic.py`, `sketch.py`, `verify_sstar.py` and `wobble_sweep.py` only print their results for comparison with the text. The table maps each section to its scripts.
